# Pharmacology Question Bank

Converted from the non-mock pharmacology question-bank JSON files in the Medical Companion repository. Correct options are marked **(Correct)**; explanations follow each option when available.

## Pharmacology

Source: `cardiovascular-pharmacology.json`

### DVT and choosing antihypertensives

1. Virchow triad (SHE) predisposes to deep venous thrombosis. What does it include?
   - **A.** Sepsis, Hypotension, Edema — Not the triad.
   - **B.** Shunting, Hypoxia, Erythrocytosis — Not the triad.
   - **C.** Stasis, Hypercoagulability, Endothelial damage **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Stenosis, Hemorrhage, Embolism — Not the triad.
   - **E.** Smoking, Hypertension, Elevated LDL — Atherosclerosis risk factors.
   - Page: 321

2. A woman on oral contraceptives develops a swollen, warm, painful calf after a long flight. Pretest probability is moderate. Which test can rule out DVT if negative?
   - **A.** Contrast venography only — Not the initial rule-out test.
   - **B.** Troponin — Cardiac marker.
   - **C.** BNP — HF marker.
   - **D.** CK-MB — Cardiac marker.
   - **E.** D-dimer **(Correct)** — Correct. High sensitivity, low specificity, so a negative result helps rule out DVT.
   - Page: 321

3. What is the imaging test of choice for suspected DVT?
   - **A.** Compression ultrasound with Doppler **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Plain x-ray — Not useful.
   - **C.** CT pulmonary angiography — Used for PE.
   - **D.** Echocardiogram — Not for DVT.
   - **E.** MRI of the leg — Not first choice.
   - Page: 321

4. Which DVTs carry the highest risk of pulmonary embolism?
   - **A.** Plantar veins — Distal.
   - **B.** Distal calf veins — Less likely to embolize; often resolve.
   - **C.** Proximal veins: femoral, iliac, popliteal **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Superficial saphenous vein — Superficial, not DVT.
   - **E.** Upper extremity cephalic vein — Superficial.
   - Page: 321

5. Which drugs are used for long-term treatment and prevention of DVT?
   - **A.** Unfractionated heparin indefinitely — Used for prophylaxis and acute management.
   - **B.** Direct anticoagulants such as rivaroxaban or apixaban **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Alteplase — Thrombolytic, not long-term.
   - **D.** Clopidogrel — Antiplatelet.
   - **E.** Aspirin alone — Not the listed long-term agent.
   - Page: 321

6. Which drug classes are first-line for primary (essential) hypertension?
   - **A.** Thiazide diuretics, ACE inhibitors, ARBs, and Ca2+ channel blockers **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** β-blockers and loop diuretics — Not first line for uncomplicated hypertension.
   - **C.** Hydralazine and methyldopa — Pregnancy.
   - **D.** α-blockers — Not first line.
   - **E.** Nitrates — Not antihypertensive first line.
   - Page: 321

7. A patient with hypertension and diabetes has microalbuminuria. Which drug class is protective against his nephropathy?
   - **A.** Loop diuretics — Not protective.
   - **B.** ACE inhibitors/ARBs **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Thiazides — Not the listed renal protection.
   - **D.** Hydralazine — Not listed.
   - **E.** β-blockers — Can mask hypoglycemia.
   - Page: 321

8. Why must β-blockers be used carefully in a hypertensive patient with diabetes on insulin?
   - **A.** They cause diabetic nephropathy — Not true.
   - **B.** They cause hyperkalemia — ACE inhibitors more so.
   - **C.** They can mask hypoglycemia symptoms **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** They cause cough — ACE inhibitors.
   - **E.** They cause hyperglycemia acutely — Not the listed concern.
   - Page: 321

9. A patient with asthma has hypertension. Which drug should be avoided and why?
   - **A.** ARBs; they cause bronchospasm — ARBs are acceptable.
   - **B.** Cardioselective β-blockers; always contraindicated — Listed as acceptable.
   - **C.** Ca2+ channel blockers; they cause wheezing — Acceptable.
   - **D.** Thiazides; they worsen asthma — Acceptable.
   - **E.** Nonselective β-blockers; β2 blockade causes bronchoconstriction **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 321

10. Why are ACE inhibitors avoided in hypertensive patients with asthma?
   - **A.** They cause hypoglycemia — Not the reason.
   - **B.** They cause bronchospasm through β2 blockade — That's nonselective β-blockers.
   - **C.** They worsen gout — Diuretics do.
   - **D.** To avoid confusion between drug-induced cough and asthma-related cough **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** They're teratogenic — Relevant in pregnancy, not asthma.
   - Page: 321

11. A pregnant woman at 30 weeks has BP 160/105 mm Hg. Which drug is appropriate?
   - **A.** Spironolactone — Not listed.
   - **B.** Lisinopril — ACE inhibitors are teratogenic; not listed.
   - **C.** Hydrochlorothiazide — Not listed.
   - **D.** Losartan — ARBs are not listed for pregnancy.
   - **E.** Nifedipine **(Correct)** — Correct. Hydralazine, methyldopa, labetalol, and nifedipine ("Hypertensive moms love nifedipine").
   - Page: 321

12. A man with hypertension and recurrent gout flares needs BP control. Which drug is especially favored?
   - **A.** Bumetanide — Loop diuretic; avoided.
   - **B.** Chlorthalidone — A thiazide-like diuretic; avoided.
   - **C.** Hydrochlorothiazide — Can cause gout flares.
   - **D.** Losartan **(Correct)** — Correct. ARBs (especially losartan), ACE inhibitors, and CCBs are preferred.
   - **E.** Furosemide — Can cause gout flares.
   - Page: 321

13. A postmenopausal woman with osteoporosis has hypertension. Which drug is preferred as monotherapy?
   - **A.** Clonidine — Not preferred.
   - **B.** Hydralazine — Not preferred.
   - **C.** Loop diuretic — ↑ Ca2+ excretion.
   - **D.** Methyldopa — Pregnancy.
   - **E.** Thiazide diuretic **(Correct)** — Correct. Thiazides ↑ Ca2+ reabsorption.
   - Page: 321

14. A patient with a pheochromocytoma is scheduled for resection. Which drug should be started first?
   - **A.** Phenoxybenzamine (α-blockade first) **(Correct)** — Correct. α-blockade precedes β-blockade.
   - **B.** Hydrochlorothiazide — Not the treatment.
   - **C.** Propranolol — β-blockade first risks unopposed α-mediated crisis.
   - **D.** Metoprolol — β-blocker first is avoided.
   - **E.** Atenolol — β-blocker first is avoided.
   - Page: 321

15. In hypertension with heart failure, which drug class provides a mortality benefit?
   - **A.** Aldosterone antagonists **(Correct)** — Correct. Also ACE inhibitors/ARBs and β-blockers in compensated HF.
   - **B.** Hydralazine alone — Not listed.
   - **C.** Loop diuretics — Improve symptoms and reduce hospitalization.
   - **D.** Clonidine — Not listed.
   - **E.** Dihydropyridine CCBs — Not listed for mortality.
   - Page: 321

16. In which setting are β-blockers contraindicated in a patient with heart failure?
   - **A.** Hypertension with HF — Used in compensated HF.
   - **B.** Post-MI — Used post-MI.
   - **C.** Compensated HFrEF — They're recommended there.
   - **D.** Cardiogenic shock **(Correct)** — Correct. They must also be used cautiously in decompensated HF.
   - **E.** Atrial fibrillation with HF — Used for rate control.
   - Page: 321

17. Which agents are used for prophylaxis and acute management of DVT?
   - **A.** Alteplase routinely — Not listed for DVT.
   - **B.** Aspirin — Not listed.
   - **C.** Warfarin alone, started without bridging — Not the listed answer.
   - **D.** Unfractionated heparin or low-molecular-weight heparins (eg, enoxaparin) **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Clopidogrel — Antiplatelet.
   - Page: 321

18. Which is an example of hypercoagulability in Virchow triad?
   - **A.** Post-operative immobility — Stasis.
   - **B.** Factor V Leiden **(Correct)** — Correct. Also OCP use and pregnancy.
   - **C.** Long-haul flight — Stasis.
   - **D.** Varicose veins — Stasis.
   - **E.** Exposed collagen after trauma — Endothelial damage.
   - Page: 321

19. A hypertensive patient with diabetes asks which antihypertensive classes are listed as acceptable. Which list is correct?
   - **A.** Hydralazine and methyldopa only — Pregnancy.
   - **B.** ACE inhibitors/ARBs, Ca2+ channel blockers, thiazides, β-blockers **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Nonselective β-blockers only — Not the listed answer.
   - **D.** Loop diuretics and α-blockers only — Not listed.
   - **E.** Nitrates and digoxin — Not antihypertensives here.
   - Page: 321

20. Why does β-blockade before α-blockade worsen pheochromocytoma?
   - **A.** β-blockers cause hypokalemia — Not the mechanism.
   - **B.** β-blockers ↑ heart rate — They ↓ HR.
   - **C.** Blocking β2-mediated vasodilation leaves α1-mediated vasoconstriction unopposed → hypertensive crisis **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** β-blockers dilate veins — Not the mechanism.
   - **E.** β-blockers ↑ catecholamine release — Not the mechanism.
   - Page: 321

### Nitrates, calcium channel blockers, hydralazine, and emergencies

21. How do nitrates relax vascular smooth muscle?
   - **A.** ↑ NO → ↑ cGMP → smooth muscle relaxation **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Inhibit PDE-3 → ↑ cAMP — Milrinone.
   - **C.** Block α1 receptors — α-blockers.
   - **D.** Open K+ channels — Not the mechanism.
   - **E.** Block L-type Ca2+ channels — CCBs.
   - Page: 322

22. Nitrates dilate which vessels predominantly, and what is the main effect?
   - **A.** Arteries >> veins; ↓ afterload — The opposite.
   - **B.** Arteries = veins; ↓ HR — Not correct.
   - **C.** Veins >> arteries; ↓ preload **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Capillaries; ↓ edema — Not correct.
   - **E.** Coronary arteries only; ↑ contractility — Not correct.
   - Page: 322

23. A man taking sildenafil for erectile dysfunction comes in with chest pain. Which drug is contraindicated?
   - **A.** Nitroglycerin **(Correct)** — Correct. Concurrent PDE-5 inhibitor use risks severe hypotension.
   - **B.** Heparin — Not contraindicated.
   - **C.** Metoprolol — Not contraindicated.
   - **D.** Atorvastatin — Not contraindicated.
   - **E.** Aspirin — Not contraindicated.
   - Page: 322

24. A factory worker exposed to nitrates has headache, dizziness, and tachycardia every Monday that fade during the week. Explanation?
   - **A.** Weekend alcohol withdrawal — Not the described mechanism.
   - **B.** Methemoglobinemia only on Mondays — Not the described mechanism.
   - **C.** Tolerance develops during the work week and is lost over the weekend; reexposure causes vasodilation symptoms **(Correct)** — Correct. "Monday disease."
   - **D.** Allergic reaction — Not related.
   - **E.** Carbon monoxide poisoning — Not related.
   - Page: 322

25. Nitrates are contraindicated in which conditions?
   - **A.** Hypertension — Not a listed contraindication.
   - **B.** Right ventricular infarction and hypertrophic cardiomyopathy **(Correct)** — Correct. Also with PDE-5 inhibitors.
   - **C.** Stable angina and pulmonary edema — These are indications.
   - **D.** Vasospastic angina — An indication.
   - **E.** ACS — An indication.
   - Page: 322

26. Which adverse effect of nitrates is treated with a β-blocker?
   - **A.** Flushing — Not treated with β-blockers.
   - **B.** Methemoglobinemia — Treated differently.
   - **C.** Tolerance — Managed with nitrate-free intervals.
   - **D.** Reflex tachycardia **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Headache — Not treated with β-blockers.
   - Page: 322

27. Which Ca2+ channel blocker has the greatest effect on vascular smooth muscle?
   - **A.** Verapamil — Greatest effect on the heart.
   - **B.** All are equal — Not equal.
   - **C.** Amlodipine (= nifedipine) **(Correct)** — Correct. Vascular: amlodipine = nifedipine > diltiazem > verapamil.
   - **D.** Diltiazem — Intermediate.
   - **E.** Nimodipine only — Not the listed order.
   - Page: 323

28. Which Ca2+ channel blocker has the greatest effect on the heart?
   - **A.** Nifedipine — Weakest cardiac effect.
   - **B.** Clevidipine — Dihydropyridine, vascular.
   - **C.** Amlodipine — Weakest cardiac effect.
   - **D.** Nicardipine — Dihydropyridine, vascular.
   - **E.** Verapamil **(Correct)** — Correct. Heart: verapamil > diltiazem > amlodipine = nifedipine.
   - Page: 323

29. A patient with subarachnoid hemorrhage is given a CCB to prevent delayed ischemia. Which drug?
   - **A.** Verapamil — Not used for SAH.
   - **B.** Amlodipine — Not for SAH.
   - **C.** Nimodipine **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Diltiazem — Not for SAH.
   - **E.** Nifedipine — Not for SAH.
   - Page: 323

30. Which CCBs are used for hypertensive urgency or emergency?
   - **A.** Nimodipine and amlodipine — Not listed.
   - **B.** All CCBs equally — Not correct.
   - **C.** Verapamil and diltiazem — Nondihydropyridines.
   - **D.** Nicardipine and clevidipine **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Nifedipine only — Not listed for emergencies.
   - Page: 323

31. A patient started on amlodipine develops ankle swelling and flushing. What explains this?
   - **A.** Heart failure from cardiac depression — Typical of nondihydropyridines.
   - **B.** Drug-induced lupus — Hydralazine.
   - **C.** AV block — Nondihydropyridines.
   - **D.** Hyperprolactinemia — Verapamil.
   - **E.** Dihydropyridine adverse effects: peripheral edema, flushing, dizziness **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 323

32. A patient on verapamil develops galactorrhea and constipation. Which adverse effects explain this?
   - **A.** Methemoglobinemia — Nitrates.
   - **B.** Cyanide toxicity — Nitroprusside.
   - **C.** Lupus-like syndrome — Hydralazine or procainamide.
   - **D.** Dihydropyridine edema — Not this.
   - **E.** Hyperprolactinemia and constipation, typical of verapamil **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 323

33. Which adverse effect is shared by Ca2+ channel blockers as a class?
   - **A.** Cyanide toxicity — Nitroprusside.
   - **B.** Pulmonary fibrosis — Amiodarone.
   - **C.** Hypoglycemia — Not listed.
   - **D.** Gingival hyperplasia **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Cough — ACE inhibitors.
   - Page: 323

34. Hydralazine lowers BP by:
   - **A.** Releasing NO directly as cyanide — Nitroprusside.
   - **B.** ↑ cGMP → smooth muscle relaxation, dilating arterioles > veins (afterload reduction) **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Blocking β1 receptors — β-blockers.
   - **D.** Inhibiting ACE — ACE inhibitors.
   - **E.** Blocking L-type Ca2+ channels — CCBs.
   - Page: 323

35. A patient on hydralazine and procainamide develops arthralgia, rash, and positive antihistone antibodies. Diagnosis?
   - **A.** Rheumatoid arthritis — Not drug-induced.
   - **B.** Serum sickness — Not the pattern.
   - **C.** Sarcoidosis — Not related.
   - **D.** Systemic sclerosis — Not drug-induced here.
   - **E.** Drug-induced lupus **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 323

36. Why is hydralazine often given with a β-blocker?
   - **A.** To ↑ BP lowering by blocking ACE — Not the reason.
   - **B.** To prevent drug-induced lupus — Not the reason.
   - **C.** To ↑ cGMP further — Not the reason.
   - **D.** To prevent compensatory reflex tachycardia **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** To prevent fluid retention — Diuretics address that.
   - Page: 323

37. A patient in hypertensive emergency receives an infusion for 3 days and develops confusion, metabolic acidosis, and an almond odor. Which drug?
   - **A.** Nitroprusside **(Correct)** — Correct. It releases cyanide.
   - **B.** Fenoldopam — Not associated.
   - **C.** Clevidipine — Not associated.
   - **D.** Nicardipine — Not associated.
   - **E.** Labetalol — Not associated.
   - Page: 323

38. Fenoldopam lowers BP in hypertensive emergency by:
   - **A.** Releasing NO — Nitroprusside.
   - **B.** Acting as a dopamine D1 receptor agonist, causing coronary, peripheral, renal, and splanchnic vasodilation with natriuresis **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Blocking α1 receptors — Not its mechanism.
   - **D.** Blocking L-type Ca2+ channels — CCBs.
   - **E.** Inhibiting neprilysin — Sacubitril.
   - Page: 323

39. Which drug is NOT listed for treating hypertensive emergency?
   - **A.** Nitroprusside — Listed.
   - **B.** Hydrochlorothiazide **(Correct)** — Correct. Thiazides are not emergency agents.
   - **C.** Fenoldopam — Listed.
   - **D.** Clevidipine — Listed.
   - **E.** Labetalol — Listed.
   - Page: 323

40. A woman with Raynaud phenomenon needs a drug to reduce vasospastic attacks. Which class is used?
   - **A.** Dihydropyridine Ca2+ channel blockers such as nifedipine **(Correct)** — Correct. Also used for hypertension and angina, including vasospastic angina.
   - **B.** Bile acid resins — Not related.
   - **C.** Loop diuretics — Not related.
   - **D.** Class IC antiarrhythmics — Not related.
   - **E.** Nonselective β-blockers — Can worsen vasospasm.
   - Page: 323

### Antianginal therapy, ranolazine, sacubitril, and lipid-lowering drugs

41. The goal of antianginal therapy is to reduce myocardial O2 consumption by lowering one or more of which determinants?
   - **A.** Cholesterol and triglycerides — Not determinants.
   - **B.** Pulmonary pressures — Not listed.
   - **C.** Coronary diameter only — Not the listed determinants.
   - **D.** End-diastolic volume, BP, HR, and contractility **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Hematocrit, PaO2, and hemoglobin — Not determinants of MVO2.
   - Page: 323

42. When nitrates are given alone, what happens to heart rate and contractility?
   - **A.** HR ↓, contractility ↑ — Not correct.
   - **B.** Both ↓ — That's β-blockers.
   - **C.** No change — Reflex changes occur.
   - **D.** Both ↑ by reflex **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** HR ↑, contractility ↓ — Not correct.
   - Page: 323

43. β-blockers given alone have which effect on end-diastolic volume and ejection time?
   - **A.** No change in either — Ejection time ↑.
   - **B.** ↓ EDV, ↓ ejection time — That's nitrates.
   - **C.** ↑ EDV, ↓ ejection time — Ejection time ↑.
   - **D.** ↓ EDV, ↑ ejection time — EDV doesn't ↓.
   - **E.** No effect or ↑ EDV; ↑ ejection time **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 323

44. Why is combining nitrates and β-blockers effective for angina?
   - **A.** Nitrates enhance β-blockade at receptors — Not the mechanism.
   - **B.** Both ↑ EDV — Nitrates ↓ EDV.
   - **C.** Both ↑ contractility — β-blockers ↓ contractility.
   - **D.** Both ↑ HR — β-blockers ↓ HR.
   - **E.** Each blocks the other's undesirable effects, giving a larger ↓ in MVO2 **(Correct)** — Correct. β-blockers blunt reflex tachycardia; nitrates offset the ↑ EDV.
   - Page: 323

45. Which drugs have antianginal effects similar to β-blockers?
   - **A.** Statins — Not antianginal in this sense.
   - **B.** Nondihydropyridine CCBs (verapamil, diltiazem) **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Dihydropyridine CCBs — Mainly vasodilators.
   - **D.** Hydralazine — Causes reflex tachycardia.
   - **E.** Nitrates — Different profile.
   - Page: 323

46. A patient with refractory angina is started on a drug that inhibits the late inward Na+ current without affecting HR or BP. Which drug?
   - **A.** Flecainide — Class IC.
   - **B.** Lidocaine — Class IB antiarrhythmic.
   - **C.** Ranolazine **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Ivabradine — Lowers HR.
   - **E.** Digoxin — Na+/K+ ATPase inhibitor.
   - Page: 324

47. Which adverse effect is associated with ranolazine?
   - **A.** Drug-induced lupus — Hydralazine.
   - **B.** Cyanide toxicity — Nitroprusside.
   - **C.** Gingival hyperplasia — CCBs.
   - **D.** QT prolongation **(Correct)** — Correct. Also constipation, dizziness, headache, nausea.
   - **E.** Pulmonary fibrosis — Amiodarone.
   - Page: 324

48. Sacubitril is a neprilysin inhibitor. Which substances does it prevent from being degraded?
   - **A.** Insulin and glucagon — Not listed.
   - **B.** Bradykinin, natriuretic peptides, angiotensin II, and substance P **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Acetylcholine and norepinephrine — Not listed.
   - **D.** Thrombin and fibrin — Not listed.
   - **E.** Renin and aldosterone — Not neprilysin substrates listed.
   - Page: 324

49. Why is sacubitril/valsartan contraindicated with ACE inhibitors?
   - **A.** Both inhibit HMG-CoA reductase — Not true.
   - **B.** Risk of angioedema, because both ↑ bradykinin **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Additive QT prolongation — Not the reason.
   - **D.** Both cause cyanide toxicity — Not true.
   - **E.** Additive hypokalemia — Not the reason.
   - Page: 324

50. Sacubitril is combined with which drug to treat HFrEF?
   - **A.** Digoxin — Not the partner.
   - **B.** Spironolactone — Can be used but isn't the combination partner.
   - **C.** Valsartan **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Hydralazine — Combined with nitrates, not sacubitril.
   - **E.** Lisinopril — Contraindicated combination.
   - Page: 324

51. Statins lower LDL by inhibiting:
   - **A.** Lipoprotein lipase — Fibrates upregulate LPL.
   - **B.** Cholesterol absorption at the brush border — Ezetimibe.
   - **C.** Hormone-sensitive lipase — Niacin.
   - **D.** PCSK9 — PCSK9 inhibitors.
   - **E.** HMG-CoA reductase → ↓ cholesterol synthesis → ↑ LDL receptor recycling **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 324

52. A patient on simvastatin and gemfibrozil develops muscle pain and very high CK. What explains this?
   - **A.** Myopathy risk, increased when statins are combined with fibrates or niacin **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Hypothyroidism from statins — Not listed.
   - **C.** Ezetimibe diarrhea — Not involved.
   - **D.** Bile acid resin malabsorption — Not involved.
   - **E.** Niacin flushing — Not the presentation.
   - Page: 324

53. A patient on cholestyramine develops easy bruising from vitamin K deficiency. Why?
   - **A.** Flushing — Niacin.
   - **B.** Bile acid resins ↓ absorption of fat-soluble vitamins and other drugs **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Hepatotoxicity — Not the mechanism.
   - **D.** Myopathy — Not related.
   - **E.** Gallstones — Fibrates.
   - Page: 324

54. Ezetimibe lowers LDL by:
   - **A.** Preventing cholesterol absorption at the small intestine brush border **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Binding bile acids — Resins.
   - **C.** Activating PPAR-α — Fibrates.
   - **D.** Inhibiting PCSK9 — PCSK9 inhibitors.
   - **E.** Inhibiting HMG-CoA reductase — Statins.
   - Page: 324

55. A patient with severe hypertriglyceridemia is given a drug that activates PPAR-α and upregulates LPL. Which adverse effect is classic?
   - **A.** Cholesterol gallstones (via inhibition of cholesterol 7α-hydroxylase) **(Correct)** — Correct. Fibrates.
   - **B.** Fat-soluble vitamin deficiency — Resins.
   - **C.** Angioedema — Sacubitril with ACE inhibitors.
   - **D.** Hyperuricemia — Niacin.
   - **E.** Flushing — Niacin.
   - Page: 325

56. A patient started on niacin develops facial flushing. How can this be reduced?
   - **A.** NSAIDs (eg, aspirin) or long-term use, since the flushing is prostaglandin mediated **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Increasing the dose rapidly — Worsens it.
   - **C.** Stop all statins — Not the solution.
   - **D.** Adding a fibrate — Not the solution.
   - **E.** Antihistamines only — Not the listed approach.
   - Page: 325

57. Which metabolic adverse effects are associated with niacin?
   - **A.** Hypocalcemia — Not listed.
   - **B.** Hyperkalemia — Not listed.
   - **C.** Hyperglycemia and hyperuricemia **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Metabolic alkalosis — Not listed.
   - **E.** Hypoglycemia and hypouricemia — The opposite.
   - Page: 325

58. Which drug produces the largest LDL reduction by preventing LDL receptor degradation and has minimal drug interactions because it isn't metabolized by CYP?
   - **A.** Fenofibrate — Mainly ↓ TG.
   - **B.** Fish oil — Mainly ↓ TG at high doses.
   - **C.** Niacin — Moderate LDL effect; CYP is not the point.
   - **D.** PCSK9 inhibitors (alirocumab, evolocumab) **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Colesevelam — Moderate LDL effect.
   - Page: 325

59. Niacin lowers lipids by:
   - **A.** Activating PPAR-α — Fibrates.
   - **B.** Blocking cholesterol absorption — Ezetimibe.
   - **C.** Inhibiting lipolysis (hormone-sensitive lipase) in adipose tissue and reducing hepatic VLDL synthesis **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Inhibiting HMG-CoA reductase — Statins.
   - **E.** Binding bile acids — Resins.
   - Page: 325

60. Bile acid resins such as cholestyramine lower LDL by which mechanism?
   - **A.** Inhibiting PCSK9 — PCSK9 inhibitors.
   - **B.** Blocking intestinal cholesterol transport at the brush border — Ezetimibe.
   - **C.** Activating PPAR-α — Fibrates.
   - **D.** Inhibiting HMG-CoA reductase — Statins.
   - **E.** Disrupting enterohepatic bile acid circulation, so the liver converts more cholesterol to bile and ↑ LDL receptor recycling **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 324

### Digoxin and class I antiarrhythmics

61. Digoxin increases contractility by directly inhibiting which target?
   - **A.** PDE-3 — Milrinone.
   - **B.** Na+/K+-ATPase **(Correct)** — Correct. This indirectly inhibits the Na+/Ca2+ exchanger, ↑ intracellular Ca2+.
   - **C.** HCN channels — Ivabradine.
   - **D.** L-type Ca2+ channels — Not the target.
   - **E.** β1 receptors — Not the target.
   - Page: 326

62. Besides positive inotropy, digoxin slows heart rate by:
   - **A.** Blocking K+ channels — Class III.
   - **B.** Blocking HCN channels — Ivabradine.
   - **C.** Releasing adenosine — Not the mechanism.
   - **D.** Blocking β1 receptors — Not the mechanism.
   - **E.** Stimulating the vagus nerve **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 326

63. An elderly patient on digoxin has nausea, diarrhea, and sees yellow halos around lights. These are signs of:
   - **A.** Hypokalemia alone — Not explained.
   - **B.** Digoxin toxicity **(Correct)** — Correct. Cholinergic effects and blurry yellow vision ("van Glow").
   - **C.** Lidocaine CNS toxicity — Different signs.
   - **D.** Amiodarone thyroid toxicity — Different signs.
   - **E.** Quinidine cinchonism — Tinnitus and headache.
   - Page: 326

64. Which arrhythmia is classic for digoxin toxicity?
   - **A.** Torsades de pointes — Associated with class IA/III.
   - **B.** Brugada pattern — Not related.
   - **C.** WPW — Not related.
   - **D.** Atrial flutter with 1:1 conduction — Not classic.
   - **E.** Atrial tachycardia with AV block **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 326

65. Which electrolyte abnormality predisposes to digoxin toxicity?
   - **A.** Hypernatremia — Not listed.
   - **B.** Hyperkalemia — Hyperkalemia indicates poor prognosis in toxicity but doesn't predispose.
   - **C.** Hypokalemia **(Correct)** — Correct. Low K+ permits digoxin binding at the K+-binding site on the Na+/K+-ATPase.
   - **D.** Hypophosphatemia — Not listed.
   - **E.** Hypercalcemia only — Not the listed answer.
   - Page: 326

66. A patient on digoxin is started on a drug that ↓ digoxin clearance and causes toxicity. Which drug?
   - **A.** Verapamil **(Correct)** — Correct. Also amiodarone and quinidine.
   - **B.** Lidocaine — Not listed.
   - **C.** Atorvastatin — Not listed.
   - **D.** Lisinopril — Not listed.
   - **E.** Furosemide — Causes hypokalemia, a different mechanism.
   - Page: 326

67. Which is part of the treatment of severe digoxin toxicity?
   - **A.** Quinidine — Raises digoxin levels.
   - **B.** Calcium infusion — Not listed.
   - **C.** Verapamil — Raises digoxin levels.
   - **D.** Anti-digoxin Fab fragments, Mg2+, and cardiac pacing **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Rapidly correct potassium to high-normal — K+ is normalized slowly.
   - Page: 326

68. In digoxin toxicity, what does hyperkalemia indicate?
   - **A.** That toxicity is mild — Not stated.
   - **B.** Renal recovery — Not stated.
   - **C.** Poor prognosis **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Good prognosis — The opposite.
   - **E.** Coexisting hypothyroidism — Not stated.
   - Page: 326

69. Class I antiarrhythmics show "use dependence." What does that mean?
   - **A.** They act only on nodal tissue — Not true.
   - **B.** Their effect is independent of heart rate — Not true.
   - **C.** Their action ↑ at faster heart rates because channels spend less time in the resting state for the drug to dissociate **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** They work better at slow heart rates — The opposite.
   - **E.** They require prior β-blockade — Not true.
   - Page: 326

70. Rank the strength of Na+ channel binding among class I subclasses.
   - **A.** IA > IB > IC — Not the order.
   - **B.** IC > IA > IB **(Correct)** — Correct. "Fast taxi CAB."
   - **C.** IB > IC > IA — Not the order.
   - **D.** IA > IC > IB — IC is strongest.
   - **E.** All equal — Not equal.
   - Page: 326

71. Which drugs are class IA antiarrhythmics?
   - **A.** Verapamil, diltiazem — Class IV.
   - **B.** Lidocaine, mexiletine — Class IB.
   - **C.** Amiodarone, sotalol — Class III.
   - **D.** Flecainide, propafenone — Class IC.
   - **E.** Quinidine, procainamide, disopyramide **(Correct)** — Correct. "The queen proclaims Diso's pyramid."
   - Page: 326

72. How do class IA drugs affect the ventricular action potential?
   - **A.** ↑ AP duration, ↑ ERP, and ↑ QT, with some K+ channel blockade **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Act only on nodal tissue — Not IA.
   - **C.** Shorten ERP — Not IA.
   - **D.** ↓ AP duration — That's IB.
   - **E.** No effect on AP duration — That's IC (minimal).
   - Page: 326

73. A patient on quinidine develops headache and tinnitus. This is called:
   - **A.** Gray baby syndrome — Chloramphenicol.
   - **B.** Disulfiram reaction — Metronidazole.
   - **C.** Red man syndrome — Vancomycin.
   - **D.** Cinchonism **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Drug-induced lupus — Procainamide.
   - Page: 326

74. Which class IA drug is associated with a reversible SLE-like syndrome?
   - **A.** Lidocaine — Class IB.
   - **B.** Procainamide **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Disopyramide — HF.
   - **D.** Flecainide — Class IC.
   - **E.** Quinidine — Cinchonism.
   - Page: 326

75. Which class IA drug is associated with heart failure?
   - **A.** Mexiletine — Class IB.
   - **B.** Quinidine — Cinchonism.
   - **C.** Disopyramide **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Propafenone — Class IC.
   - **E.** Procainamide — Lupus-like syndrome.
   - Page: 326

76. Which drugs are class IB antiarrhythmics?
   - **A.** Ibutilide, dofetilide — Class III.
   - **B.** Metoprolol, esmolol — Class II.
   - **C.** Quinidine, procainamide — Class IA.
   - **D.** Flecainide, propafenone — Class IC.
   - **E.** Lidocaine, mexiletine **(Correct)** — Correct. "I'd Buy Liddy's Mexican tacos."
   - Page: 326

77. A patient develops sustained VT shortly after an acute MI. Which class I drug preferentially affects ischemic or depolarized tissue and is "Best post-MI"?
   - **A.** Disopyramide — Class IA.
   - **B.** Propafenone — Class IC.
   - **C.** Flecainide — Contraindicated post-MI.
   - **D.** Lidocaine **(Correct)** — Correct. Class IB.
   - **E.** Quinidine — Class IA.
   - Page: 326

78. Class IB drugs are also used for which specific arrhythmias?
   - **A.** Digitalis-induced arrhythmias **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Atrial flutter rate control — Not listed.
   - **C.** Torsades de pointes — Magnesium.
   - **D.** WPW reentry only — Not listed.
   - **E.** Bradyarrhythmias — Not listed.
   - Page: 326

79. Which adverse effects are typical of class IB drugs?
   - **A.** Lupus-like syndrome — Procainamide.
   - **B.** CNS stimulation/depression and cardiovascular depression **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Gingival hyperplasia — CCBs.
   - **D.** Cinchonism — Quinidine.
   - **E.** Pulmonary fibrosis — Amiodarone.
   - Page: 326

80. A patient with a prior MI and structural heart disease is considered for flecainide for atrial fibrillation. Why is this inappropriate?
   - **A.** Flecainide is ineffective for SVT — It's used for SVTs.
   - **B.** Flecainide prolongs QT markedly — Minimal AP effect.
   - **C.** Flecainide causes cyanide toxicity — Nitroprusside.
   - **D.** Class IC drugs are proarrhythmic and contraindicated in structural and ischemic heart disease **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Flecainide causes hypothyroidism — Amiodarone.
   - Page: 327

### Class II–IV antiarrhythmics, adenosine, magnesium, and ivabradine

81. Class IC drugs significantly prolong the ERP in which tissues?
   - **A.** All tissues equally — Not true.
   - **B.** Purkinje and ventricular tissue — No effect there.
   - **C.** Atrial appendage only — Not stated.
   - **D.** AV node and accessory bypass tracts **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** SA node only — Not stated.
   - Page: 327

82. Class II antiarrhythmics (β-blockers) suppress abnormal pacemakers by:
   - **A.** ↓ cAMP and ↓ Ca2+ currents, ↓ slope of phase 4 **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Blocking fast Na+ channels — Class I.
   - **C.** Opening K+ channels — Adenosine.
   - **D.** Blocking K+ channels — Class III.
   - **E.** ↑ slope of phase 4 — The opposite.
   - Page: 327

83. Which ECG interval is most prolonged by β-blockers?
   - **A.** ST segment — Not affected.
   - **B.** PR interval **(Correct)** — Correct. The AV node is particularly sensitive.
   - **C.** QRS — Not mainly.
   - **D.** QT — Class III.
   - **E.** None — PR ↑.
   - Page: 327

84. Which β-blocker is very short acting?
   - **A.** Atenolol — Long acting.
   - **B.** Carvedilol — Not very short.
   - **C.** Propranolol — Not very short acting.
   - **D.** Timolol — Not very short.
   - **E.** Esmolol **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 327

85. A patient with vasospastic angina is started on propranolol and his angina worsens. Why?
   - **A.** Propranolol causes coronary steal — Not the mechanism.
   - **B.** Propranolol causes hyperkalemia — Not the issue.
   - **C.** Propranolol can exacerbate vasospasm in vasospastic angina **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Propranolol prolongs QT — Not the issue.
   - **E.** Propranolol ↑ heart rate — It ↓ HR.
   - Page: 327

86. A child ingests her grandfather's metoprolol and is bradycardic and hypotensive. Which treatment mnemonic applies?
   - **A.** SAD — AS symptoms.
   - **B.** GAS: Glucagon, Atropine, Saline **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** BEAR — AR causes.
   - **D.** CHALK — Muscle metabolites.
   - **E.** ABCD — AF management.
   - Page: 327

87. Which β-blocker adverse effect is listed?
   - **A.** Blue-gray skin deposits — Amiodarone.
   - **B.** Gingival hyperplasia — CCBs.
   - **C.** Cyanide toxicity — Nitroprusside.
   - **D.** Exacerbation of COPD and asthma, impotence, and masking hypoglycemia **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Cinchonism — Quinidine.
   - Page: 327

88. Which drugs are class III antiarrhythmics?
   - **A.** Esmolol, metoprolol — Class II.
   - **B.** Amiodarone, ibutilide, dofetilide, sotalol **(Correct)** — Correct. "AIDS."
   - **C.** Diltiazem, verapamil — Class IV.
   - **D.** Lidocaine, mexiletine — Class IB.
   - **E.** Adenosine, magnesium — Other antiarrhythmics.
   - Page: 328

89. Class III antiarrhythmics affect the action potential by:
   - **A.** ↑ conduction velocity — Not the effect.
   - **B.** ↓ AP duration and ↓ ERP — The opposite.
   - **C.** ↑ AP duration, ↑ ERP, ↑ QT interval **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** ↓ slope of phase 4 only — Class II.
   - **E.** ↓ slope of phase 0 only — Class I.
   - Page: 328

90. Before starting a patient on amiodarone, which baseline tests should be checked?
   - **A.** PFTs, LFTs, and TFTs **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** CBC, CK, and lipase — Not the listed tests.
   - **C.** BNP and troponin — Not listed.
   - **D.** Urinalysis and lipids — Not listed.
   - **E.** Serum digoxin and lithium levels — Not listed.
   - Page: 328

91. A patient on long-term amiodarone develops thyroid dysfunction. Why does amiodarone affect the thyroid?
   - **A.** It blocks TSH receptors — Not the stated mechanism.
   - **B.** It destroys thyroid tissue by radiation — Not true.
   - **C.** It is about 40% iodine by weight and can cause hypothyroidism or hyperthyroidism **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** It blocks iodine uptake completely — Not the stated mechanism.
   - **E.** It is a thyroid hormone analog — Not the stated mechanism.
   - Page: 328

92. A man on amiodarone develops blue-gray skin discoloration with photodermatitis and corneal deposits. Why?
   - **A.** Cyanosis from methemoglobinemia — Not this.
   - **B.** Silver toxicity — Argyria, unrelated.
   - **C.** Iron deposition — Not the mechanism.
   - **D.** Amiodarone acts as a hapten, causing corneal and skin deposits **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Copper deposition — Wilson disease.
   - Page: 328

93. Amiodarone is unusual among antiarrhythmics because it:
   - **A.** Is lipophilic and has class I, II, III, and IV effects **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Is very short acting — It's long acting.
   - **C.** Shortens QT — It prolongs QT.
   - **D.** Has only class III effects — It has class I–IV effects.
   - **E.** Has no pulmonary toxicity — Causes pulmonary fibrosis.
   - Page: 328

94. Which class III drug carries a risk of torsades and excessive β blockade?
   - **A.** Lidocaine — Class IB.
   - **B.** Sotalol **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Dofetilide — Torsades, but no β blockade.
   - **D.** Ibutilide — Torsades only.
   - **E.** Amiodarone — Torsades risk is lower.
   - Page: 328

95. Class IV antiarrhythmics (diltiazem, verapamil) affect conduction by:
   - **A.** ↑ conduction velocity and ↓ PR — The opposite.
   - **B.** Blocking fast Na+ channels — Class I.
   - **C.** Opening K+ channels — Adenosine.
   - **D.** ↓ conduction velocity, ↑ ERP, and ↑ PR interval **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** ↑ QT markedly — Class III.
   - Page: 328

96. A patient with PSVT receives adenosine. How does adenosine slow AV nodal conduction?
   - **A.** Blocks β1 receptors — Class II.
   - **B.** Inhibits Na+/K+-ATPase — Digoxin.
   - **C.** Blocks fast Na+ channels — Class I.
   - **D.** Blocks K+ channels — Class III.
   - **E.** ↑ K+ efflux, hyperpolarizing cells and ↓ ICa **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 328

97. A patient who drinks large amounts of coffee and takes theophylline receives adenosine for PSVT, but it has little effect. Why?
   - **A.** Caffeine blocks K+ channels — Not the mechanism.
   - **B.** Adenosine is long acting and accumulates — Very short acting (~15 sec).
   - **C.** Caffeine and theophylline antagonize adenosine receptors, blunting its effect **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Caffeine and theophylline accelerate adenosine's renal excretion — Not the mechanism; adenosine is cleared within seconds regardless.
   - **E.** Theophylline ↑ adenosine metabolism only — Not the mechanism.
   - Page: 328

98. After adenosine, a patient briefly reports flushing, chest pain, and a sense of impending doom. These effects last about:
   - **A.** 24 hours — Too long.
   - **B.** Permanently — Not true.
   - **C.** 3 days — Too long.
   - **D.** 2 hours — Too long.
   - **E.** About 15 seconds, since adenosine is very short acting **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 328

99. Magnesium is effective in which arrhythmic conditions?
   - **A.** Sick sinus syndrome — Not listed.
   - **B.** First-degree AV block — Not listed.
   - **C.** WPW only — Not listed.
   - **D.** Atrial flutter and PSVT — Not listed.
   - **E.** Torsades de pointes and digoxin toxicity **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 328

100. A patient with chronic HFrEF in sinus rhythm is started on a drug that selectively inhibits the funny current (If). He later notices visual brightness. Which drug?
   - **A.** Ivabradine **(Correct)** — Correct. Luminous phenomena are characteristic.
   - **B.** Verapamil — Class IV.
   - **C.** Ranolazine — Late Na+ current.
   - **D.** Amiodarone — Corneal deposits.
   - **E.** Digoxin — Yellow vision.
   - Page: 328

## Pharmacology

Source: `endocrine-pharmacology.json`

### Insulin, metformin, pioglitazone, and secretagogues

101. A 14-year-old girl is admitted in DKA. Which insulin preparation is given intravenously?
   - **A.** Detemir — Long-acting.
   - **B.** Glargine — Long-acting, given subcutaneously.
   - **C.** NPH — Intermediate-acting, not given IV.
   - **D.** Degludec — Very long-acting.
   - **E.** Regular insulin **(Correct)** — Correct. Regular (short-acting) insulin is preferred IV for DKA, hyperkalemia and stress hyperglycemia.
   - Page: 358

102. A patient with K+ 7.1 mEq/L and peaked T waves is given IV regular insulin. Why is IV glucose given with it?
   - **A.** To prevent hypoglycemia from the insulin **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Glucose stimulates aldosterone — Not the reason.
   - **C.** Glucose lowers potassium more than insulin — Insulin is the active drug.
   - **D.** Glucose blocks K+ release from cells — Not the reason.
   - **E.** Glucose binds potassium in plasma — Not correct.
   - Page: 358

103. Which insulin has the fastest onset, with essentially no lag, and is used at mealtimes?
   - **A.** Glargine — Long acting.
   - **B.** Lispro **(Correct)** — Correct. Lispro, aspart and glulisine are rapid acting.
   - **C.** Degludec — Very long acting.
   - **D.** NPH — Intermediate.
   - **E.** Regular — Short acting with some lag.
   - Page: 358

104. Which insulin is classified as very long acting?
   - **A.** Degludec **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Glargine — Long acting.
   - **C.** Aspart — Rapid acting.
   - **D.** Detemir — Long acting.
   - **E.** NPH — Intermediate.
   - Page: 358

105. A patient injecting insulin into the same site notices a lumpy area of fat there. Which adverse effect is this?
   - **A.** Edema — Pioglitazone.
   - **B.** Lactic acidosis — Metformin.
   - **C.** Hypersensitivity — Usually urticarial.
   - **D.** Lipodystrophy **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Hypoglycemia — A systemic effect.
   - Page: 358

106. What is first-line pharmacotherapy for type 2 diabetes?
   - **A.** Acarbose — Not first line.
   - **B.** Insulin glargine — Not first line in most patients.
   - **C.** Metformin **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Pioglitazone — Not first line.
   - **E.** Glyburide — Not first line.
   - Page: 358

107. A woman with gestational diabetes fails diet and exercise. Which treatment is listed in First Aid?
   - **A.** Pioglitazone — Not used.
   - **B.** SGLT2 inhibitor — Not used.
   - **C.** Insulin replacement **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Glyburide only — Not the listed choice.
   - **E.** No treatment — Treatment is needed.
   - Page: 358

108. Metformin lowers blood glucose mainly by:
   - **A.** Activating PPAR-γ — Pioglitazone.
   - **B.** Blocking SGLT2 — Gliflozins.

   - **D.** Inhibiting DPP-4 — Gliptins.
   - **E.** Decreasing hepatic gluconeogenesis via mGPD inhibition and AMPK activation **(Correct)** — Correct.

109. A 70-year-old man on metformin develops sepsis with hypotension. Which serious adverse effect is he at risk for?
   - **A.** Hypoglycemia — Metformin alone rarely causes it.
   - **B.** Lactic acidosis **(Correct)** — Correct. Risk rises with renal insufficiency, cirrhosis, sepsis or hypoperfusion.
   - **C.** Pancreatitis — GLP-1 agonists.
   - **D.** Heart failure — Pioglitazone.
   - **E.** Disulfiram-like reaction — First-generation sulfonylureas.

110. A patient on long-term metformin has macrocytic anemia and paresthesias. Which deficiency is likely?
   - **A.** Iron — Microcytic.
   - **B.** Vitamin D — Not related.
   - **C.** Folate — Not the classic association.
   - **D.** Thiamine — Not related.
   - **E.** Vitamin B12 **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 359

111. Which effect on body weight is typical of metformin?
   - **A.** Weight gain — Sulfonylureas, insulin and pioglitazone.
   - **B.** Severe edema — Pioglitazone.
   - **C.** Cachexia — Not correct.
   - **D.** Weight loss **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** No effect ever — Typically modest loss.

112. Pioglitazone acts on which target?

   - **B.** K_ATP channel — Sulfonylureas.
   - **C.** GLP-1 receptor — Exenatide and liraglutide.
   - **D.** α-glucosidase — Acarbose.

   - **B.** Pioglitazone **(Correct)** — Correct. Causes weight gain, edema and heart failure.
   - **C.** Sitagliptin — Weight neutral.

   - **E.** Empagliflozin — Reduces volume.

114. Which adverse effect is associated with pioglitazone?
   - **A.** Pancreatitis — GLP-1 agonists.

   - **C.** Increased risk of fractures **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Genital mycotic infections — SGLT2 inhibitors.
   - **E.** Disulfiram-like reaction — First-generation sulfonylureas.

115. A patient starts pioglitazone and sees no change in glucose after 1 week. What is the best explanation?
   - **A.** Pioglitazone has a delayed onset of action (several weeks) **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Pioglitazone needs insulin injections to work — Not correct.
   - **C.** It works only in type 1 diabetes — Not correct.
   - **D.** The dose must be given IV — Not correct.
   - **E.** Pioglitazone is ineffective in type 2 diabetes — It is effective.

116. Glipizide increases insulin secretion by:
   - **A.** Activating GLP-1 receptors — Not correct.
   - **B.** Opening K+ channels — That would hyperpolarize the cell.
   - **C.** Inhibiting DPP-4 — Not correct.
   - **D.** Blocking SGLT2 — Not correct.
   - **E.** Closing ATP-sensitive K+ channels in β cells, causing depolarization and Ca2+ influx **(Correct)** — Correct.

117. An older man with CKD on glyburide is found unconscious with glucose 30 mg/dL. Why is his risk higher?
   - **A.** Glyburide raises glucagon — Not correct.
   - **B.** Glyburide blocks counter-regulation via α receptors — Not correct.
   - **C.** Glyburide causes lactic acidosis — Metformin.

   - **A.** Hypoglycemia only — Not the full picture.
   - **B.** Disulfiram-like reaction with first-generation sulfonylureas **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Carcinoid syndrome — Not related.

   - **E.** Allergic reaction to sulfa — Not this pattern.

   - **A.** Biguanides — Metformin.

   - **D.** Gliflozins — SGLT2 inhibitors.
   - **E.** Glitazones — PPAR-γ agonists.

120. Which adverse effect is common to insulin, sulfonylureas and meglitinides?
   - **A.** Fractures — Pioglitazone.
   - **B.** Glucosuria — SGLT2 inhibitors.

131. An elderly patient on empagliflozin becomes dizzy on standing. Which adverse effect?
   - **A.** Dehydration with orthostatic hypotension **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Hyperkalemia — Not the listed effect.
   - **C.** Bradycardia — Not related.
   - **D.** Lactic acidosis — Metformin.
   - **E.** Hypoglycemia — Less likely.
   - Page: 359

132. Why are SGLT2 inhibitors less effective in advanced CKD?
   - **A.** They block insulin — Not the reason.
   - **B.** They are cleared too fast — Not the reason.
   - **C.** They cause hyperkalemia — Not the reason.
   - **D.** Efficacy depends on filtered glucose, which falls with GFR **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** They require liver activation — Not the reason.
   - Page: 359

133. Acarbose lowers postprandial glucose by:
   - **A.** Activating AMPK — Metformin.
   - **B.** Slowing gastric emptying via GLP-1 — Not correct.
   - **C.** Blocking renal glucose reabsorption — SGLT2 inhibitors.
   - **D.** Inhibiting intestinal brush-border α-glucosidases, delaying carbohydrate absorption **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Increasing insulin secretion — Not correct.
   - Page: 359

134. What is the main adverse effect of acarbose?
   - **A.** GI upset and bloating **(Correct)** — Correct. Undigested carbohydrate ferments in the colon.
   - **B.** Edema — Pioglitazone.
   - **C.** Lactic acidosis — Metformin.
   - **D.** Hypoglycemia — Not typical alone.
   - **E.** Pancreatitis — GLP-1 agonists.
   - Page: 359

135. Pramlintide is an analog of which hormone?
   - **A.** Somatostatin — Octreotide.
   - **B.** Amylin **(Correct)** — Correct. Decreases glucagon, slows gastric emptying and increases satiety.
   - **C.** Glucagon — Not correct.
   - **D.** GIP — Not correct.
   - **E.** GLP-1 — Exenatide and liraglutide.
   - Page: 359

136. Which adverse effects are listed for pramlintide?
   - **A.** Disulfiram-like reaction — First-generation sulfonylureas.
   - **B.** Hypoglycemia and nausea **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Lactic acidosis and B12 deficiency — Metformin.
   - **D.** Genital infections — SGLT2 inhibitors.
   - **E.** Edema and fractures — Pioglitazone.
   - Page: 359

137. Which drug class causes weight loss in type 2 diabetes?
   - **A.** Insulin — Weight gain.
   - **B.** SGLT2 inhibitors **(Correct)** — Correct. Glucose (and calories) are lost in urine.
   - **C.** Pioglitazone — Weight gain.
   - **D.** Meglitinides — Weight gain.
   - **E.** Sulfonylureas — Weight gain.
   - Page: 359

138. Which drug class is weight neutral?
   - **A.** Pioglitazone — Causes weight gain.
   - **B.** Insulin — Causes weight gain.
   - **C.** DPP-4 inhibitors **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** GLP-1 agonists — Cause weight loss.
   - **E.** Sulfonylureas — Cause weight gain.
   - Page: 359

139. A GLP-1 agonist can increase the risk of which biliary problem?
   - **A.** Biliary atresia — Not related.
   - **B.** Cholangiocarcinoma — Not listed.
   - **C.** Primary sclerosing cholangitis — Not related.
   - **D.** Gallbladder disease (cholelithiasis or cholecystitis) **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Choledochal cyst — Not related.
   - Page: 359

140. α-glucosidase inhibitors are not recommended in which condition?
   - **A.** Gout — Not a contraindication.
   - **B.** Hypertension — Not a contraindication.

### Thyroid drugs, pituitary drugs, fludrocortisone, cinacalcet, sevelamer, and K+ binders

141. A woman in her first trimester of pregnancy has Graves disease. Which drug is preferred, and why?
   - **A.** Radioactive iodine — Contraindicated in pregnancy.
   - **B.** PTU, because methimazole is teratogenic in the first trimester **(Correct)** — Correct. Methimazole is used in the 2nd and 3rd trimesters because of PTU hepatotoxicity.
   - **C.** No treatment is needed — Hyperthyroidism should be treated.
   - **D.** Methimazole, because it is safer for the liver — Methimazole is teratogenic in early pregnancy.
   - **E.** Levothyroxine — Treats hypothyroidism.
   - Page: 360

142. A patient on methimazole develops fever and sore throat. Which serious adverse effect should be checked for first?
   - **A.** Agranulocytosis **(Correct)** — Correct. Check a CBC with differential.
   - **B.** Lactic acidosis — Metformin.
   - **C.** Hypothyroidism — Not the urgent concern.
   - **D.** Aplasia cutis — Teratogenic effect in the fetus.
   - **E.** Hepatotoxicity — Possible, but fever and sore throat suggest neutropenia.
   - Page: 360

143. Which adverse effect is specifically associated with PTU?
   - **A.** Ototoxicity — Not related.
   - **B.** Pulmonary fibrosis — Amiodarone.
   - **C.** Aplasia cutis — Methimazole in the fetus.
   - **D.** Gingival hyperplasia — Phenytoin.
   - **E.** ANCA-positive vasculitis and hepatotoxicity **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 360

144. A newborn whose mother took methimazole early in pregnancy has a scalp defect without skin. What is this?
   - **A.** Neural tube defect — Valproate.
   - **B.** Phocomelia — Thalidomide.
   - **C.** Cleft palate — Phenytoin.
   - **D.** Aplasia cutis **(Correct)** — Correct. A possible methimazole teratogenic effect.
   - **E.** Ebstein anomaly — Lithium.
   - Page: 360

145. A woman's TSH rises despite a stable levothyroxine dose. She recently started ferrous sulfate taken at the same time. Why?
   - **A.** Iron increases deiodinase — Not correct.
   - **B.** Iron stimulates TSH directly — Not correct.
   - **C.** Iron causes thyroiditis — Not correct.
   - **D.** Iron increases TBG — Not the reason.
   - **E.** Ferrous sulfate decreases levothyroxine absorption **(Correct)** — Correct. So do antacids and bile acid resins.
   - Page: 360

146. Which drug is a T3 replacement?
   - **A.** Propranolol — β-blocker.
   - **B.** Liothyronine **(Correct)** — Correct (lio = 3 letters).
   - **C.** Potassium iodide — Not replacement.
   - **D.** Methimazole — Thionamide.
   - **E.** Levothyroxine — T4.
   - Page: 360

147. Which adverse effects suggest levothyroxine overdose?
   - **A.** Bradycardia and cold intolerance — Underreplacement.
   - **B.** Weight gain and constipation — Underreplacement.
   - **C.** Hyperkalemia — Not related.
   - **D.** Hypoglycemia — Not typical.
   - **E.** Tachycardia, heat intolerance, tremor and arrhythmias **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 360

148. A patient with thyrotoxicosis may be secretly taking levothyroxine. Which approach helps distinguish exogenous from endogenous hyperthyroidism?
   - **A.** Measuring calcitonin — Not related.
   - **B.** TSH receptor antibodies, radioactive iodine uptake and thyroid blood flow on ultrasound **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Measuring TSH alone — TSH is low in both.
   - **D.** Chest CT — Not useful.
   - **E.** Measuring cortisol — Not related.
   - Page: 360

149. Which drug is used for SIADH by blocking ADH at the V2 receptor?
   - **A.** Conivaptan **(Correct)** — Correct. Conivaptan and tolvaptan are V2 antagonists.
   - **B.** Desmopressin — A V2 agonist.
   - **C.** Octreotide — Somatostatin analog.
   - **D.** Oxytocin — Induces labor.
   - **E.** Fludrocortisone — Mineralocorticoid.
   - Page: 360

150. Demeclocycline, used for SIADH, belongs to which drug class?
   - **A.** Macrolide — Not correct.
   - **B.** Fluoroquinolone — Not correct.
   - **C.** Tetracycline **(Correct)** — Correct. It interferes with ADH signaling.
   - **D.** Aminoglycoside — Not correct.
   - **E.** Sulfonamide — Not correct.
   - Page: 360

151. A patient with von Willebrand disease is having a dental extraction. Which hormone analog can raise vWF and factor VIII levels?
   - **A.** Oxytocin — Not used.
   - **B.** Growth hormone — Not used.
   - **C.** Conivaptan — ADH antagonist.
   - **D.** Octreotide — Not used.
   - **E.** Desmopressin **(Correct)** — Correct. Also used for central DI, enuresis and hemophilia A.
   - Page: 360

152. A girl with Turner syndrome has short stature. Which hormone is used to increase her height?
   - **A.** Estrogen only — Used for puberty, not height.
   - **B.** Thyroxine — Only if hypothyroid.
   - **C.** Growth hormone **(Correct)** — Correct. GH is used for GH deficiency and Turner syndrome.
   - **D.** Oxytocin — Not used.
   - **E.** Testosterone — Not used.
   - Page: 360

153. A woman has postpartum hemorrhage from uterine atony. Which hormone is given?
   - **A.** Oxytocin **(Correct)** — Correct. Stimulates uterine contractions and controls hemorrhage.
   - **B.** Octreotide — Not used.
   - **C.** Desmopressin — Not used.
   - **D.** Prolactin — Not used.
   - **E.** Vasopressin — Not first line for atony.

154. A patient with cirrhosis has bleeding esophageal varices. Which somatostatin analog reduces portal pressure?
   - **A.** Conivaptan — Not used.
   - **B.** Desmopressin — Not used.
   - **C.** Propranolol only acutely — Used for prophylaxis.
   - **D.** Octreotide **(Correct)** — Correct. Also used in acromegaly, carcinoid syndrome, gastrinoma and glucagonoma.
   - **E.** Terlipressin only — Used in some settings but not listed here.
   - Page: 360

155. Fludrocortisone is used for:
   - **A.** Mineralocorticoid replacement in primary adrenal insufficiency **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Hyperaldosteronism — Would worsen it.
   - **C.** Secondary adrenal insufficiency only — Aldosterone is preserved in secondary disease.
   - **D.** Cushing syndrome — Not used.
   - **E.** SIADH — Not used.
   - Page: 360

156. A patient with Addison disease on fludrocortisone develops ankle edema and dyspnea. Which adverse effect?
   - **A.** Agranulocytosis — Not related.
   - **B.** Hypoglycemia — Not typical.
   - **C.** Lactic acidosis — Not related.
   - **D.** Edema and exacerbation of heart failure from fluid retention **(Correct)** — Correct ("fluidrocortisone retains fluid").
   - **E.** Hypokalemia only — Not the full picture.
   - Page: 360

157. A dialysis patient has secondary hyperparathyroidism. Which drug lowers PTH by sensitizing the calcium-sensing receptor?
   - **A.** Calcitriol only — Lowers PTH by another mechanism.
   - **B.** Sevelamer — Phosphate binder.
   - **C.** Alendronate — Bisphosphonate.
   - **D.** Cinacalcet **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Teriparatide — PTH analog.
   - Page: 361

158. Which adverse effect is expected with cinacalcet?
   - **A.** Hypokalemia — Not listed.
   - **B.** Hyperphosphatemia — Not the listed effect.
   - **C.** Hypocalcemia **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Lactic acidosis — Not related.
   - **E.** Hypercalcemia — Not expected.
   - Page: 361

159. A patient with CKD has phosphate 7.8 mg/dL. Which nonabsorbable drug binds dietary phosphate?
   - **A.** Calcitonin — Not used.
   - **B.** Sevelamer **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Cinacalcet — Lowers PTH.
   - **D.** Furosemide — Not used.
   - **E.** Patiromer — Binds potassium.
   - Page: 361

160. A patient with hyperkalemia receives patiromer. How does it work, and what is a risk?
   - **A.** Shifts K+ into cells; hypoglycemia — That is insulin.
   - **B.** Stabilizes the myocardium; arrhythmia — Calcium gluconate stabilizes membranes.
   - **C.** Binds K+ in the colon in exchange for other cations; hypokalemia **(Correct)** — Correct. Also sodium polystyrene sulfonate and zirconium cyclosilicate.
   - **D.** Increases renal K+ excretion; hyponatremia — Not correct.
   - **E.** Blocks aldosterone; hyperkalemia — Not correct.
   - Page: 361

### Acid suppression and mucosal protection

161. A 60-year-old man taking warfarin and phenytoin starts an over-the-counter drug for heartburn. His INR rises and he becomes ataxic. Which drug did he take?
   - **A.** Famotidine — Wrong. Relatively free of P-450 effects.
   - **B.** Sucralfate — Wrong. Not a P-450 inhibitor.
   - **C.** Cimetidine **(Correct)** — Correct. A potent cytochrome P-450 inhibitor with many interactions.
   - **D.** Omeprazole — Wrong. Less interaction potential here.
   - **E.** Calcium carbonate — Wrong. Chelates some drugs but not this pattern.
   - Page: 406

162. A man taking a heartburn drug long term develops gynecomastia and decreased libido. Which drug is responsible?
   - **A.** Bismuth — Wrong. Not typical.
   - **B.** Famotidine — Wrong. Not typical.
   - **C.** Misoprostol — Wrong. Not typical.
   - **D.** Pantoprazole — Wrong. Not typical.
   - **E.** Cimetidine **(Correct)** — Correct. Antiandrogenic effects (prolactin release, gynecomastia, impotence).
   - Page: 406

163. H2-receptor blockers reduce acid secretion by which mechanism?

   - **C.** Giardia — Wrong. Not the listed association.

   - **E.** Salmonella only — Wrong. Not the listed association.

## Autonomic Drugs

Source: `gp-pharmacology-autonomic.json`

### Drug effect modifications and autonomic receptors

221. What does an additive drug interaction mean?
   - **A.** Response falls with repeat dosing — Wrong. Tachyphylactic.
   - **B.** One drug enables the other — Wrong. Permissive.
   - **C.** The effect is less than the sum — Wrong. Antagonistic.
   - **D.** The effect of two substances together equals the sum of their individual effects **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** The effect is greater than the sum — Wrong. Synergistic.
   - Page: 234

222. Which pair is given as an example of an additive effect?
   - **A.** Aspirin and acetaminophen **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Morphine with naloxone — Wrong. Antagonistic.
   - **C.** Cortisol with catecholamines — Wrong. Permissive.
   - **D.** Clopidogrel with aspirin — Wrong. Synergistic.
   - **E.** Carbidopa with levodopa — Wrong. Potentiation.
   - Page: 234

223. What does a permissive interaction mean?
   - **A.** Both have equal effects — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** The combined effect is less than the sum — Wrong. Antagonistic.
   - **C.** The combined effect exceeds the sum — Wrong. Synergistic.
   - **D.** Response falls after repeated doses — Wrong. Tachyphylactic.
   - **E.** One substance is required for the full effect of another **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 234

224. Which example illustrates a permissive effect?
   - **A.** Morphine with naloxone — Wrong. Antagonistic.
   - **B.** Oxymetazoline used repeatedly — Wrong. Tachyphylactic.
   - **C.** Aspirin and acetaminophen — Wrong. Additive.
   - **D.** Clopidogrel with aspirin — Wrong. Synergistic.
   - **E.** Cortisol on catecholamine responsiveness **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 234

225. What does a synergistic interaction mean?
   - **A.** The effect of two substances together is greater than the sum of their individual effects **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** The effect equals the sum — Wrong. Additive.
   - **C.** Response falls with repetition — Wrong. Tachyphylactic.
   - **D.** The effect is less than the sum — Wrong. Antagonistic.
   - **E.** One drug has no action alone — Wrong. Potentiation.
   - Page: 234

226. Which pair illustrates synergism?
   - **A.** Morphine with naloxone — Wrong. Antagonistic.
   - **B.** Cortisol with catecholamines — Wrong. Permissive.
   - **C.** Clopidogrel with aspirin **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Aspirin and acetaminophen — Wrong. Additive.
   - **E.** Carbidopa with levodopa — Wrong. Potentiation.
   - Page: 234

227. How does potentiation differ from synergism?
   - **A.** The second drug is more potent alone — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** There is no difference — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** The second drug has no therapeutic action alone but enhances the first **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** The combined effect is less than the sum — Wrong. Antagonistic.
   - **E.** Both drugs act at the same receptor — Wrong. Not the definition.
   - Page: 234

   - **B.** It blocks nicotinic receptors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** It blocks alpha-1 receptors — Wrong. Tamsulosin.
   - **D.** It blocks M3 receptors — Wrong. Oxybutynin.
   - **E.** It stimulates M3 receptors, contracting the detrusor and emptying the bladder **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 236

262. A woman with urgency incontinence is given oxybutynin. How does it work?
   - **A.** It stimulates M3 receptors — Wrong. Bethanechol.
   - **B.** It blocks beta-3 receptors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** It blocks alpha-1 receptors — Wrong. Tamsulosin.
   - **D.** It blocks M3 receptors, relaxing the detrusor and reducing overactivity **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** It stimulates beta-3 receptors — Wrong. Mirabegron.
   - Page: 236

263. How does mirabegron treat urgency incontinence?
   - **A.** By blocking nicotinic receptors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** By stimulating beta-3 receptors, relaxing the detrusor and raising bladder capacity **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** By blocking M3 receptors — Wrong. Oxybutynin.
   - **D.** By blocking alpha-1 receptors — Wrong. Tamsulosin.
   - **E.** By stimulating M3 receptors — Wrong. Bethanechol.
   - Page: 236

264. How does tamsulosin help benign prostatic hyperplasia?
   - **A.** It stimulates beta-3 receptors — Wrong. Mirabegron.
   - **B.** It shrinks the prostate gland itself — Wrong. Not the stated mechanism.
   - **C.** It stimulates alpha-1 receptors — Wrong. Opposite.
   - **D.** It blocks M3 receptors — Wrong. Oxybutynin.
   - **E.** It blocks alpha-1 receptors, relaxing smooth muscle in the bladder neck and prostate and reducing obstruction **(Correct)** — Correct.

265. What effects do alpha-1 receptors have on vascular and visceral smooth muscle?
   - **A.** Vasoconstriction and smooth muscle contraction **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Increased lipolysis — Wrong. Beta-3.
   - **C.** Vasodilation and relaxation — Wrong. Beta-2.
   - **D.** Increased heart rate — Wrong. Beta-1.
   - **E.** Bronchodilation — Wrong. Beta-2.

266. What do alpha-2 receptors do in the pancreas, at presynaptic terminals and in salivary glands?

   - **B.** They inhibit insulin secretion, neurotransmitter release and salivary secretion **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** They stimulate all three — Wrong. Opposite.
   - **D.** They raise heart rate — Wrong. Beta-1.
   - **E.** They raise lipolysis — Wrong. Beta-3.
   - Page: 236

267. What do beta-1 receptors do in the heart and kidney?

   - **B.** They inhibit insulin secretion — Wrong. Alpha-2.
   - **C.** They lower heart rate — Wrong. M2.
   - **D.** They cause bronchodilation — Wrong. Beta-2.
   - **E.** They raise heart rate and contractility, and raise renin secretion **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 236

268. What do beta-2 receptors do in bronchioles, liver, arteries and pancreas?
   - **A.** Only increased heart rate — Wrong. Incomplete.
   - **B.** Inhibition of insulin secretion — Wrong. Alpha-2.
   - **C.** Bronchoconstriction and vasoconstriction — Wrong. Opposite.
   - **D.** Only increased lipolysis — Wrong. Beta-3.
   - **E.** Bronchodilation, glycogenolysis with glucose release, vasodilation and stimulation of insulin secretion **(Correct)** — Correct.

269. What do beta-3 receptors do in adipose tissue?
   - **A.** They cause vasoconstriction — Wrong. Alpha-1.
   - **B.** They reduce lipolysis — Wrong. Opposite.
   - **C.** They increase lipolysis **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** They stimulate insulin release — Wrong. Beta-2.
   - **E.** They raise heart rate — Wrong. Beta-1.
   - Page: 236

270. Which adrenergic receptors in cardiac muscle raise heart rate and contractility?
   - **A.** Only beta-3 — Wrong. Adipose.
   - **B.** Only beta-1 — Wrong. Incomplete.
   - **C.** Only alpha-1 — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Both beta-1 and beta-2 **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Only alpha-2 — Wrong. Not correct.

   - **B.** Gs — Wrong. Beta receptors.
   - **C.** Gq **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Gi — Wrong. Alpha-2.
   - **E.** It is a ligand-gated channel — Wrong. Not correct.

272. What are the major functions of the alpha-1 receptor?

   - **B.** Increased heart rate — Wrong. Beta-1.
   - **C.** Increased gastric acid — Wrong. H2.

   - **B.** Gq — Wrong. Alpha-1.

   - **E.** Gt — Wrong. Not correct.

   - **B.** Bronchodilation — Wrong. Beta-2.
   - **C.** Increased heart rate and renin — Wrong. Beta-1.
   - **D.** Reduced sympathetic outflow, reduced insulin release, reduced lipolysis, increased platelet aggregation and reduced aqueous humor production **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Increased gut peristalsis — Wrong. M3.

   - **C.** Increased gastric acid — Wrong. H2.
   - **D.** Increased thermogenesis in skeletal muscle — Wrong. Beta-3.
   - **E.** Bronchodilation and vasodilation — Wrong. Beta-2.

278. What are the major functions of the beta-3 receptor?
   - **A.** Increased lipolysis, increased thermogenesis in skeletal muscle and increased bladder relaxation **(Correct)** — Correct.

   - **E.** Increased renin release — Wrong. Beta-1.

### Cholinomimetic agents and anticholinesterase poisoning

281. Which conditions can be worsened by cholinomimetic agents?

   - **B.** Urinary retention — Wrong. These drugs treat it.
   - **C.** Glaucoma — Wrong. These drugs treat it.
   - **D.** Myasthenia gravis — Wrong. These drugs treat it.
   - **E.** COPD, asthma and peptic ulcers **(Correct)** — Correct.

282. What is bethanechol used for, and which receptors does it act on?
   - **A.** Myasthenia gravis; nicotinic receptors — Wrong. Pyridostigmine.
   - **B.** Alzheimer disease; central receptors — Wrong. Donepezil.
   - **C.** Asthma diagnosis; muscarinic receptors — Wrong. Methacholine.
   - **D.** Glaucoma; nicotinic receptors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Urinary retention; muscarinic receptors only, with no nicotinic activity **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 239

283. What is carbachol used for?

   - **D.** Treating xerostomia — Wrong. Pilocarpine.
   - **E.** Urinary retention — Wrong. Bethanechol.
   - Page: 239

284. What is methacholine used for?
   - **A.** As a challenge test to diagnose asthma **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Treating urinary retention — Wrong. Bethanechol.

   - **D.** Treating Alzheimer disease — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Treating glaucoma — Wrong. Pilocarpine.

   - **D.** Myasthenia gravis — Wrong. Pyridostigmine.
   - **E.** Asthma diagnosis — Wrong. Methacholine.

   - **A.** Glaucoma; it is lipophilic — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Anticholinergic toxicity; it crosses freely — Wrong. Physostigmine.
   - **C.** Postoperative and neurogenic ileus, urinary retention, myasthenia gravis and reversal of neuromuscular blockade; its positive charge prevents blood-brain barrier penetration **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Alzheimer disease; it is uncharged — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Motion sickness — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 239

289. What is pyridostigmine used for, and which drugs control its adverse effects?

   - **B.** Anticholinergic toxicity; pralidoxime — Wrong. Not correct.

   - **E.** Glaucoma; timolol — Wrong. Not correct.

290. Which anticholinesterase is the antidote for anticholinergic toxicity, and why?
   - **A.** Pyridostigmine, because it is long acting — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Physostigmine, because it is uncharged and freely crosses the blood-brain barrier **(Correct)** — Correct.

   - **D.** Pralidoxime, because it regenerates the enzyme — Wrong. Organophosphates.
   - **E.** Neostigmine, because it is charged — Wrong. Does not reach the brain.

291. What causes anticholinesterase poisoning, and in whom is it usually seen?
   - **A.** Antihistamines; in children — Wrong. Anticholinergic toxicity.
   - **B.** Organophosphates such as fenthion, parathion and malathion, which irreversibly inhibit acetylcholinesterase; usually in farmers **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Beta-blockers; in older adults — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Atropine; in surgical patients — Wrong. Opposite.
   - **E.** Opioids; in hospital patients — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 239

   - **B.** Dry mouth, flushed skin and mydriasis — Wrong. Anticholinergic toxicity.

   - **D.** Only respiratory depression — Wrong. CNS effect.
   - **E.** Only hypertension — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 239

293. Which drug reverses the muscarinic effects of organophosphate poisoning, and how?
   - **A.** Atropine, as a competitive inhibitor that also crosses the blood-brain barrier to relieve CNS symptoms **(Correct)** — Correct.

   - **C.** Physostigmine, by raising acetylcholine — Wrong. Would worsen it.
   - **D.** Neostigmine — Wrong. Would worsen it.
   - **E.** Pralidoxime, by blocking receptors — Wrong. It regenerates the enzyme.

   - **A.** Lacrimation — Wrong. Muscarinic.

   - **C.** Salivation — Wrong. Muscarinic.
   - **D.** Miosis — Wrong. Muscarinic.
   - **E.** Bronchospasm — Wrong. Muscarinic.
   - Page: 239

295. How does pralidoxime work in organophosphate poisoning?
   - **A.** It blocks nicotinic receptors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** It raises acetylcholine — Wrong. Opposite.
   - **C.** It blocks muscarinic receptors — Wrong. Atropine.
   - **D.** It binds the organophosphate in the gut — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** It regenerates acetylcholinesterase by dephosphorylation if given early **(Correct)** — Correct.

296. Why must pralidoxime be given with atropine?

   - **E.** To prevent transient worsening of symptoms **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 239

   - **B.** Only at high doses — Wrong. Not stated.
   - **C.** Yes, freely — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** No, not readily **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Only with atropine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 239

   - **A.** Hyperthermia alone — Wrong. Anticholinergic toxicity.
   - **B.** Mydriasis — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** No CNS effects occur — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Respiratory depression, lethargy, seizures and coma **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Agitation and hallucinations only — Wrong. Anticholinergic toxicity.
   - Page: 239

   - **D.** Carbachol **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Bethanechol — Wrong. Bladder selective.

## Pharmacokinetics & Pharmacodynamics

Source: `gp-pharmacology-pk-pd.json`

### Enzyme kinetics and inhibitors

501. What does Km represent?
   - **A.** The rate constant of elimination — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** The slope of the Lineweaver-Burk plot — Wrong. Km over Vmax.
   - **C.** The maximum reaction velocity — Wrong. Vmax.
   - **D.** The enzyme concentration — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** The substrate concentration at which an enzyme reaches half its maximum velocity **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 228

502. How does Km relate to enzyme affinity for substrate?
   - **A.** Inversely; a high Km means low affinity **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** There is no relationship — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Directly; a high Km means high affinity — Wrong. Reversed.
   - **D.** It depends on enzyme concentration — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** It depends on temperature only — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 228

503. What is Vmax directly proportional to?
   - **A.** Km — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Substrate concentration — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Enzyme concentration **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Inhibitor concentration — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Temperature alone — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 228

504. A sigmoidal rather than hyperbolic enzyme curve usually indicates what?
   - **A.** Zero-order kinetics — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Noncompetitive inhibition — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Enzyme denaturation — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Competitive inhibition — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Positive cooperativity in substrate binding **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 228

505. Which enzyme is given as an example of positive cooperativity?
   - **A.** Lactate dehydrogenase — Wrong. Not the stated example.
   - **B.** Aspartate transcarbamoylase **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Hexokinase — Wrong. Not the stated example.
   - **D.** Glucokinase — Wrong. Not the stated example.
   - **E.** Catalase — Wrong. Not the stated example.
   - Page: 228

506. On a Lineweaver-Burk plot, what does a y-intercept closer to zero indicate?
   - **A.** A lower Vmax — Wrong. Reversed.
   - **B.** A higher Vmax **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Nothing useful — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** A higher Km — Wrong. That is the x-axis.
   - **E.** A lower enzyme concentration — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 228

507. On a Lineweaver-Burk plot, what does an x-intercept closer to zero indicate?
   - **A.** A higher Vmax — Wrong. That is the y-axis.
   - **B.** A higher Km **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** A lower Vmax — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** A lower Km — Wrong. Reversed.
   - **E.** Higher affinity — Wrong. High Km means low affinity.

508. How do reversible competitive inhibitors appear on a Lineweaver-Burk plot compared with the uninhibited line?
   - **A.** The lines cross each other **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** The lines are identical — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** The line is vertical — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** The lines are parallel — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** The lines do not cross — Wrong. Noncompetitive.
   - Page: 228

509. Do reversible competitive inhibitors resemble the substrate, and can increased substrate overcome them?
   - **A.** No to the first, yes to the second — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** No to both — Wrong. Noncompetitive.
   - **C.** Yes to both **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Neither applies — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Yes to the first, no to the second — Wrong. Irreversible competitive.

510. Where do noncompetitive inhibitors bind?
   - **A.** The cofactor only — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** The substrate itself — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** The product — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** The active site — Wrong. Competitive inhibitors.
   - **E.** An allosteric site **(Correct)** — Correct.

511. How does a reversible competitive inhibitor affect Vmax and Km?
   - **A.** Vmax is unchanged and Km rises **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Both are unchanged — Wrong. Not correct.

   - **D.** Both fall — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Both rise — Wrong. Not correct.

512. How does an irreversible competitive inhibitor affect Vmax and Km?
   - **A.** Both rise — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Km falls only — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Both fall — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Vmax unchanged, Km raised — Wrong. Reversible competitive.
   - **E.** Vmax falls and Km is unchanged **(Correct)** — Correct.

513. How does a noncompetitive inhibitor affect Vmax and Km?
   - **A.** Vmax unchanged, Km raised — Wrong. Reversible competitive.
   - **B.** Both unchanged — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Both rise — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Vmax falls and Km is unchanged **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Km falls — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 228

514. Can increased substrate overcome an irreversible competitive inhibitor?
   - **A.** Only if the enzyme is in excess — Wrong. Not correct.

   - **D.** No **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Yes — Wrong. Only reversible ones.
   - Page: 228

515. Do noncompetitive inhibitors resemble the substrate?
   - **A.** Only at the allosteric site — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Yes — Wrong. Competitive inhibitors do.
   - **C.** Only partially — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** No **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** It varies by enzyme — Wrong. Not correct.

   - **A.** Neither — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Only duration of action — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Potency **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Both potency and efficacy — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Efficacy — Wrong. The other two types.

517. In pharmacodynamic terms, what do irreversible competitive and noncompetitive inhibitors reduce?
   - **A.** Efficacy **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Potency — Wrong. Reversible competitive.
   - **C.** Only affinity — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Only half-life — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Neither — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 228

518. Which inhibitors raise Km?
   - **A.** Noncompetitive inhibitors — Wrong. Km unchanged.
   - **B.** All inhibitors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Reversible competitive inhibitors **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Irreversible competitive inhibitors — Wrong. Km unchanged.
   - **E.** None of them — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 228

519. Which inhibitors bind the active site?
   - **A.** Only irreversible ones — Wrong. Incomplete.
   - **B.** All three types — Wrong. Not correct.

   - **D.** Both reversible and irreversible competitive inhibitors **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Noncompetitive inhibitors only — Wrong. Allosteric.

520. Most enzymatic reactions follow which kinetic pattern?
   - **A.** A linear curve — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** A hyperbolic curve, which is Michaelis-Menten kinetics **(Correct)** — Correct.
