### Bioavailability, volume of distribution, clearance and half-life

521. What is bioavailability?
   - **A.** The fraction of an administered drug that reaches the systemic circulation **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** The rate of elimination — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** The volume of plasma cleared per unit time — Wrong. Clearance.
   - **D.** The time to eliminate half the drug — Wrong. Half-life.
   - **E.** The ratio of drug amount to plasma concentration — Wrong. Volume of distribution.
   - Page: 229

522. What is the bioavailability of an intravenous dose?
   - **A.** 100% **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** It varies by drug — Wrong. Not for IV.
   - **C.** 50% — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** It cannot be measured — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** 75% — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 229

523. Why is oral bioavailability usually below 100%?
   - **A.** Because of tissue storage — Wrong. Not the stated reason.
   - **B.** Because of zero-order kinetics — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Because of plasma protein binding — Wrong. Not the stated reason.
   - **D.** Because of renal clearance — Wrong. Not the stated reason.
   - **E.** Because of incomplete absorption and first-pass metabolism **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 229

524. How is bioavailability calculated from plasma concentration data?
   - **A.** From the half-life alone — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** From the volume of distribution — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** From the elimination constant — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** From the peak concentration alone — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** From the area under the curve, comparing oral and intravenous doses **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 229

525. What is the volume of distribution?
   - **A.** The fraction reaching circulation — Wrong. Bioavailability.
   - **B.** A theoretical value relating the amount of drug in the body to plasma concentration **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** The time to steady state — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** The volume of plasma cleared per unit time — Wrong. Clearance.
   - **E.** The total body water — Wrong. Not the definition.
   - Page: 229

526. How do liver and kidney disease affect the volume of distribution?
   - **A.** They only affect half-life — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** They decrease it — Wrong. Opposite.
   - **C.** They increase it, because protein binding falls **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** They have no effect — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** They only affect clearance — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 229

527. Hemodialysis is most effective for drugs with which property?
   - **A.** Extensive tissue binding — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** A long half-life only — Wrong. Not the stated property.
   - **C.** A high volume of distribution — Wrong. Opposite.
   - **D.** A low volume of distribution **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** High lipid solubility — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 229

528. Which drugs have a low volume of distribution, and where do they stay?
   - **A.** Only drugs given intravenously — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Any drug given orally — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Large or charged molecules and those bound to plasma protein; they stay intravascular **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Small hydrophilic molecules; they reach extracellular fluid — Wrong. Medium Vd.
   - **E.** Small lipophilic molecules; they reach all tissues — Wrong. High Vd.
   - Page: 229

529. Which drugs have a medium volume of distribution?
   - **A.** Small lipophilic molecules — Wrong. High Vd.
   - **B.** Small hydrophilic molecules distributing in extracellular fluid **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Only intravenous drugs — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Plasma protein bound drugs — Wrong. Low Vd.
   - **E.** Large charged molecules — Wrong. Low Vd.
   - Page: 229

530. Which drugs have a high volume of distribution?
   - **A.** Small lipophilic molecules, especially those bound to tissue protein **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Only oral drugs — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Plasma protein bound drugs — Wrong. Low Vd.
   - **D.** Small hydrophilic molecules — Wrong. Medium Vd.
   - **E.** Large charged molecules — Wrong. Low Vd.
   - Page: 229

531. What is clearance?
   - **A.** The amount of drug in the body — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** The theoretical distribution volume — Wrong. Volume of distribution.
   - **C.** The fraction reaching circulation — Wrong. Bioavailability.
   - **D.** The volume of plasma cleared of drug per unit time **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** The time to eliminate half the drug — Wrong. Half-life.
   - Page: 229

532. How is clearance related to volume of distribution and the elimination constant?
   - **A.** Clearance equals half-life times volume — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Clearance equals the volume of distribution times the elimination constant **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Clearance is independent of both — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Clearance equals the constant divided by volume — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Clearance equals volume of distribution divided by the constant — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 229

533. Which organ dysfunctions impair clearance?
   - **A.** Only cardiac dysfunction — Wrong. Incomplete.
   - **B.** Pulmonary dysfunction only — Wrong. Not stated.
   - **C.** Only renal dysfunction — Wrong. Incomplete.
   - **D.** Only hepatic dysfunction — Wrong. Incomplete.
   - **E.** Cardiac, hepatic or renal dysfunction **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 229

534. What is half-life?
   - **A.** The time to reach steady state — Wrong. Takes several half-lives.
   - **B.** The time to reach peak concentration — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** The time required to eliminate half the drug from the body **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** The fraction absorbed — Wrong. Bioavailability.
   - **E.** The volume cleared per unit time — Wrong. Clearance.
   - Page: 229

535. How is half-life related to volume of distribution and clearance?
   - **A.** Half-life equals 0.7 times clearance — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Half-life is independent of both — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Half-life equals clearance divided by volume — Wrong. Inverted.
   - **D.** Half-life equals 0.7 times the volume of distribution divided by clearance **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Half-life equals volume times clearance — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 229

536. What is steady state?
   - **A.** The point at which all drug is eliminated — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Complete absorption — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** The peak concentration after one dose — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** The moment of first administration — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** A dynamic equilibrium where the rate of elimination equals the rate of administration, so concentration stays constant **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 229

537. In first-order kinetics, how many half-lives does a constant infusion take to reach steady state?
   - **A.** 8 to 10 — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** 20 — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** 4 to 5 **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** 1 to 2 — Wrong. Not enough.
   - **E.** 2 to 3 — Wrong. Not enough.
   - Page: 229

538. How many half-lives are needed to reach 90% of the steady-state level?
   - **A.** 7 — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** 2.2 — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** 5.5 — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** 3.3 **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** 1.5 — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 229

539. After three half-lives of first-order elimination, what percentage of drug remains?
   - **A.** 3% — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** 12.5% **(Correct)** — Correct.

   - **D.** 25% — Wrong. Two half-lives.
   - **E.** 6.25% — Wrong. Four half-lives.
   - Page: 229

540. After four half-lives of first-order elimination, what percentage remains?
   - **A.** 6.25% **(Correct)** — Correct.

   - **D.** 12.5% — Wrong. Three half-lives.

### Age-related changes, drug metabolism and elimination kinetics

541. Which pharmacokinetic process is mostly unaffected by aging?
   - **A.** Metabolism — Wrong. Falls.
   - **B.** Excretion — Wrong. Falls.
   - **C.** Absorption **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Distribution — Wrong. Changes with body water and fat.
   - **E.** All change markedly — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 229

542. How does falling total body water with age affect hydrophilic drugs?
   - **A.** They are not absorbed — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Their volume of distribution rises — Wrong. Opposite.
   - **C.** Their half-life falls — Wrong. Not the stated effect.
   - **D.** Their volume of distribution falls, so concentration rises **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** They are cleared faster — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 229

543. How does rising total body fat with age affect lipophilic drugs?
   - **A.** Their volume of distribution rises, so half-life lengthens **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** They are not absorbed — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Their concentration rises immediately — Wrong. Not the stated effect.
   - **D.** They are cleared faster — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Their volume of distribution falls — Wrong. Opposite.
   - Page: 229

544. How does aging affect hepatic drug handling?
   - **A.** Reduced hepatic mass and blood flow lower first-pass metabolism and hepatic clearance **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** First-pass metabolism rises — Wrong. Opposite.
   - **C.** Only bile flow changes — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** The liver is unaffected — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Hepatic clearance rises — Wrong. Opposite.
   - Page: 229

545. Which phase of drug metabolism declines first with age?
   - **A.** Phase II — Wrong. Relatively preserved.
   - **B.** Phase I **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Neither declines — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** It varies randomly — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Both equally — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 229

546. How does aging affect renal drug excretion?
   - **A.** GFR is unchanged — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Only tubular secretion rises — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Excretion stops entirely — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Renal clearance rises — Wrong. Opposite.
   - **E.** Reduced renal mass and blood flow lower GFR and renal clearance **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 229

547. Why do older patients often need reduced drug doses?
   - **A.** Altered passage of drugs through the body means standard doses give higher plasma concentrations **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** They absorb more drug — Wrong. Absorption is unaffected.
   - **C.** They have more body water — Wrong. Opposite.
   - **D.** Their receptors are absent — Wrong. Not the stated reason.
   - **E.** Their clearance rises — Wrong. Opposite.
   - Page: 229

548. Which reactions make up phase I of drug metabolism?
   - **A.** Only conjugation — Wrong. Phase II.
   - **B.** Methylation, glucuronidation, acetylation and sulfation — Wrong. Phase II.
   - **C.** Only excretion — Wrong. Not metabolism.
   - **D.** Reduction, oxidation and hydrolysis, predominantly through cytochrome P-450 **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Only oxidation — Wrong. Incomplete.
   - Page: 230

549. Which reactions make up phase II of drug metabolism?
   - **A.** Reduction, oxidation and hydrolysis — Wrong. Phase I.
   - **B.** Only oxidation — Wrong. Phase I.
   - **C.** Methylation, glucuronidation, acetylation and sulfation **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Only renal filtration — Wrong. Not metabolism.
   - **E.** Only hydrolysis — Wrong. Phase I.
   - Page: 230

550. What kind of metabolite does phase I produce?
   - **A.** A very polar hydrophilic metabolite — Wrong. Phase II.
   - **B.** An entirely lipophilic product — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Only inactive products — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Only active products — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** A slightly polar metabolite, which may be active or inactive **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 230

551. What kind of metabolite does phase II produce?
   - **A.** An unchanged drug — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** A very polar hydrophilic metabolite, except the acetylated one **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** A lipophilic metabolite — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** A slightly polar metabolite — Wrong. Phase I.
   - **E.** Reactive oxygen metabolites only — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 230

552. Must phase I and phase II reactions occur in order?
   - **A.** Yes, always phase I then phase II — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** No; phase I can follow phase II, or occur as a single reaction **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** They always occur together — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Only phase I occurs in humans — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Yes, always phase II first — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 230

553. Why do slow acetylators have more adverse effects from certain drugs?
   - **A.** Their rate of metabolism is reduced, as with isoniazid **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Their renal clearance is higher — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** They lack cytochrome P-450 entirely — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** They have a lower volume of distribution — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** They absorb more drug — Wrong. Not the mechanism.
   - Page: 230

554. Which phase of metabolism do geriatric patients lose first?
   - **A.** Neither — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Phase II — Wrong. Preserved.
   - **C.** Only sulfation — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Both at once — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Phase I **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 230

555. What characterizes zero-order elimination?
   - **A.** A constant fraction is eliminated per unit time — Wrong. First order.
   - **B.** Elimination stops entirely — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Half-life is constant — Wrong. First order.
   - **D.** A constant amount of drug is eliminated per unit time regardless of concentration **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Concentration falls exponentially — Wrong. First order.
   - Page: 230

556. How does plasma concentration change over time in zero-order elimination?
   - **A.** It fluctuates randomly — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Exponentially — Wrong. First order.
   - **C.** Linearly **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** It rises — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** It stays constant — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 230

557. Which drugs follow zero-order elimination at high or toxic concentrations?
   - **A.** Digoxin and warfarin — Wrong. Not the stated drugs.
   - **B.** Phenytoin, ethanol and aspirin **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** All drugs — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Penicillin and gentamicin — Wrong. Not the stated drugs.
   - **E.** Metformin and insulin — Wrong. Not the stated drugs.
   - Page: 230

558. Zero-order elimination is also called what?
   - **A.** Saturable absorption — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** First-pass metabolism — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Flow-dependent elimination — Wrong. First order.
   - **D.** Capacity-limited elimination **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Renal clearance — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 230

559. What characterizes first-order elimination?
   - **A.** Increasing half-life with time — Wrong. Zero order.
   - **B.** No elimination at low concentration — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Linear fall in concentration — Wrong. Zero order.

   - **E.** A constant fraction eliminated per unit time, proportional to concentration **(Correct)** — Correct.

560. How does half-life behave in zero-order compared with first-order elimination?
   - **A.** Both have constant half-lives — Wrong. Not correct.

   - **C.** In zero order the half-life lengthens as concentration rises, while in first order it stays constant **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Neither has a measurable half-life — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Both shorten with time — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 230

575. What is drug efficacy?
   - **A.** The amount of drug needed for a given effect — Wrong. Potency.
   - **B.** The time to maximum effect — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** The ratio of toxic to effective dose — Wrong. Therapeutic index.
   - **D.** The fraction reaching circulation — Wrong. Bioavailability.
   - **E.** The maximal effect a drug can produce, its intrinsic activity, shown by Emax on the y-axis **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 232

576. Is efficacy related to potency?
   - **A.** Yes, they always change together — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Only for antagonists — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Yes, inversely — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Only for agonists — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** No; efficacious drugs can have high or low potency **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 232

577. How do partial agonists compare with full agonists in efficacy?
   - **A.** They have equal efficacy — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** They have less efficacy **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** They have no efficacy — Wrong. That would be an antagonist.
   - **D.** Efficacy cannot be compared — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** They have more efficacy — Wrong. Opposite.
   - Page: 232

578. What is drug potency?
   - **A.** The volume of distribution — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** The amount of drug needed for a given effect, shown by EC50 on the x-axis **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** The half-life — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** The toxic dose — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** The maximal effect achievable — Wrong. Efficacy.
   - Page: 232

579. What does a left shift of the dose-response curve indicate?
   - **A.** Nothing about the drug — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** A higher Emax — Wrong. That is efficacy.
   - **C.** A lower EC50, meaning higher potency and less drug needed **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** A lower Emax — Wrong. That is efficacy.
   - **E.** A higher EC50 and lower potency — Wrong. Right shift.
   - Page: 232

580. A drug with a higher Emax than another has what?
   - **A.** Lower toxicity — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** A longer half-life — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** A higher therapeutic index — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Greater potency — Wrong. That is EC50.
   - **E.** Greater efficacy **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 232

### Receptor binding and therapeutic index

581. How does a competitive antagonist affect an agonist's potency and efficacy?
   - **A.** Efficacy falls while potency is unchanged — Wrong. Noncompetitive antagonist.
   - **B.** Neither changes — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Potency falls while efficacy is unchanged **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Both rise — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Both fall — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 233

582. Can a competitive antagonist be overcome by raising the agonist concentration?
   - **A.** Only with a partial agonist — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Only at low doses — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Only in vitro — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Yes **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** No — Wrong. That is a noncompetitive antagonist.
   - Page: 233

583. Which pair illustrates an agonist with a competitive antagonist at the GABA-A receptor?
   - **A.** Morphine with buprenorphine — Wrong. Partial agonist.
   - **B.** Histamine with diphenhydramine — Wrong. Inverse agonist.
   - **C.** Norepinephrine with phenoxybenzamine — Wrong. Noncompetitive.
   - **D.** Diazepam with flumazenil **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Acetylcholine with atropine — Wrong. Not the stated example.
   - Page: 233

584. How does a noncompetitive antagonist affect an agonist's potency and efficacy?
   - **A.** Potency falls, efficacy unchanged — Wrong. Competitive antagonist.
   - **B.** Both fall — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Potency is unchanged while efficacy falls **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Both rise — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Neither changes — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 233

585. Which pair illustrates an agonist with a noncompetitive antagonist at alpha receptors?
   - **A.** Norepinephrine with phenoxybenzamine **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Morphine with buprenorphine — Wrong. Partial agonist.
   - **C.** Histamine with diphenhydramine — Wrong. Inverse agonist.
   - **D.** Dopamine with haloperidol — Wrong. Not the stated example.
   - **E.** Diazepam with flumazenil — Wrong. Competitive.
   - Page: 233

586. Can a noncompetitive antagonist be overcome by raising the agonist concentration?
   - **A.** No **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Yes — Wrong. That is competitive.
   - **C.** Only at high doses — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Only with a second agonist — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Only over time — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 233

587. How does a partial agonist acting alone compare with a full agonist?
   - **A.** Its potency is independent and its efficacy is lower **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Its potency is lower and efficacy unchanged — Wrong. Competitive antagonist pattern.
   - **C.** Both are higher — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** It has no effect at all — Wrong. That is an antagonist.
   - **E.** Both are unchanged — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 233

588. Where does a partial agonist act relative to the full agonist?
   - **A.** At the same site **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** At an allosteric site — Wrong. Noncompetitive antagonist.
   - **C.** On the enzyme that degrades the agonist — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Intracellularly only — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** At a different receptor entirely — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 233

589. Which pair illustrates a full agonist and a partial agonist at opioid mu receptors?
   - **A.** Epinephrine with propranolol — Wrong. Not the stated example.
   - **B.** Norepinephrine with phenoxybenzamine — Wrong. Noncompetitive.
   - **C.** Histamine with diphenhydramine — Wrong. Inverse agonist.
   - **D.** Diazepam with flumazenil — Wrong. Competitive antagonist.
   - **E.** Morphine with buprenorphine **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 233

590. What is an inverse agonist?
   - **A.** A drug that raises receptor number — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** A drug with no intrinsic activity — Wrong. Antagonist.
   - **C.** A drug that blocks the agonist competitively — Wrong. Competitive antagonist.
   - **D.** A drug that binds a constitutively active receptor and reduces its activity, giving the opposite effect of an agonist **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** A drug with lower efficacy at the same site — Wrong. Partial agonist.
   - Page: 233

591. Which drugs are given as examples of inverse agonists?
   - **A.** Flumazenil — Wrong. Competitive antagonist.
   - **B.** H1 antihistamines such as diphenhydramine **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Phenoxybenzamine — Wrong. Noncompetitive antagonist.
   - **D.** Morphine — Wrong. Full agonist.
   - **E.** Buprenorphine — Wrong. Partial agonist.
   - Page: 233

592. What effect does an inverse agonist have on efficacy?
   - **A.** It is unchanged — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** It cannot be measured — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** It is reduced but still positive — Wrong. Partial agonist.
   - **D.** It is doubled — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** It is negative **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 233

593. How is the therapeutic index calculated?
   - **A.** Emax divided by EC50 — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** The median toxic dose divided by the median effective dose **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** The median effective dose divided by the toxic dose — Wrong. Inverted.
   - **D.** Half-life times clearance — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Clearance divided by volume of distribution — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 233

594. What does a higher therapeutic index mean?
   - **A.** The drug is more potent — Wrong. Not the same.
   - **B.** The drug is safer **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** The drug is more efficacious — Wrong. Not the same.
   - **D.** The drug needs more frequent monitoring — Wrong. Opposite.
   - **E.** The drug has a longer half-life — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 233

595. Which drugs have a low therapeutic index and need frequent monitoring?
   - **A.** Penicillin and amoxicillin — Wrong. Wide index.
   - **B.** Paracetamol at normal doses — Wrong. Not the stated group.
   - **C.** Omeprazole — Wrong. Not the stated group.
   - **D.** Metformin — Wrong. Not the stated group.
   - **E.** Warfarin, theophylline, digoxin, antiepileptic drugs and lithium **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 233

596. What is the therapeutic window?
   - **A.** The time to steady state — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** The duration of action — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** The time between doses — Wrong. Dosing interval.
   - **D.** The ratio of toxic to effective dose — Wrong. Therapeutic index.
   - **E.** The range of drug concentrations that can safely and effectively treat disease **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 233

597. In animal studies, which value often replaces the median toxic dose?
   - **A.** The Emax — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** The median effective dose — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** The lethal median dose **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** The EC50 — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** The pKa — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 233

598. A drug given with a competitive antagonist shows a right-shifted curve with the same maximum. What does this confirm?
   - **A.** Efficacy fell while potency was preserved — Wrong. Noncompetitive.
   - **B.** Potency fell while efficacy was preserved **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** The drug became an inverse agonist — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** The drug became a partial agonist — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Both fell — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 233

599. A drug given with a second agent shows a lower maximum but an unchanged EC50. Which agent was added?
   - **A.** A full agonist — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Nothing was added — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** A competitive antagonist — Wrong. Shifts EC50.
   - **D.** A noncompetitive antagonist **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** An inverse agonist on a different receptor — Wrong. Not the stated pattern.

600. Why do drugs with a narrow therapeutic index require careful dosing?
   - **A.** They are poorly absorbed — Wrong. Not the reason.

   - **C.** The toxic and effective doses are close together **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** They have no antidote — Wrong. Not the reason.

## Toxicities & Adverse Effects

Source: `gp-pharmacology-toxicities.json`

### Seafood and plant toxins

601. A man develops oral burning, facial flushing and itching soon after eating tuna. He is misdiagnosed as having a fish allergy. What caused his symptoms?
   - **A.** Tetrodotoxin blocking sodium channels — Wrong. Pufferfish.
   - **B.** Grayanotoxin from honey — Wrong. Plant toxin.
   - **C.** Ciguatoxin opening sodium channels — Wrong. Reef fish.
   - **D.** Histamine formed when bacterial histidine decarboxylase converted histidine in spoiled fish **(Correct)** — Correct. Scombroid poisoning.
   - **E.** A true IgE-mediated allergy — Wrong. Misdiagnosis.
   - Page: 246

602. Which fish are classically responsible for scombroid poisoning?
   - **A.** Pufferfish — Wrong. Tetrodotoxin.
   - **B.** Spoiled dark-meat fish such as tuna, mahi-mahi, mackerel and bonito **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Barracuda and snapper — Wrong. Ciguatoxin.
   - **D.** Shellfish — Wrong. Not the stated source.
   - **E.** Freshwater fish — Wrong. Not the stated source.
   - Page: 246

603. How is scombroid poisoning treated?
   - **A.** Antihistamines, with albuterol and possibly epinephrine **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Atropine — Wrong. Not the stated treatment.
   - **C.** Supportive care only — Wrong. Tetrodotoxin and ciguatoxin.
   - **D.** Physostigmine — Wrong. Anticholinergic toxicity.
   - **E.** Activated charcoal only — Wrong. Not the stated treatment.
   - Page: 246

604. How does tetrodotoxin act, and where does it come from?
   - **A.** It binds fast voltage-gated sodium channels in nerve tissue and prevents depolarization; it comes from pufferfish **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** It blocks muscarinic receptors; from plants — Wrong. Atropine.
   - **C.** It releases histamine; from spoiled tuna — Wrong. Scombroid.
   - **D.** It inhibits the sodium-potassium pump — Wrong. Cardiac glycosides.
   - **E.** It opens sodium channels; from reef fish — Wrong. Ciguatoxin.
   - Page: 246

605. Which symptoms follow tetrodotoxin poisoning, and how is it treated?
   - **A.** Nausea, diarrhea, paresthesias, weakness, dizziness and loss of reflexes; treatment is supportive **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Dry mouth and delirium; physostigmine — Wrong. Anticholinergic.
   - **C.** Ascending paralysis; charcoal — Wrong. Coniine.
   - **D.** Reversal of hot and cold sensation; supportive — Wrong. Ciguatoxin.
   - **E.** Facial flushing and urticaria; antihistamines — Wrong. Scombroid.
   - Page: 246

606. A diver develops vomiting, numbness around the mouth and finds that hot drinks feel cold after eating barracuda. Which toxin is responsible?
   - **A.** Aconitine — Wrong. Monkshood.
   - **B.** Grayanotoxin — Wrong. Mad honey.
   - **C.** Tetrodotoxin — Wrong. Pufferfish.
   - **D.** Ciguatoxin **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Histamine — Wrong. Scombroid.
   - Page: 246

607. How does ciguatoxin act, and which fish carry it?
   - **A.** It blocks muscarinic receptors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** It blocks sodium channels; pufferfish — Wrong. Tetrodotoxin.
   - **C.** It opens sodium channels, causing depolarization; reef fish such as barracuda, snapper and moray eel **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** It inhibits acetylcholinesterase — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** It releases histamine; tuna — Wrong. Scombroid.
   - Page: 246

   - **A.** No cardiac effects — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Ventricular tachycardia — Wrong. Aconitine.
   - **C.** Bradycardia, heart block and hypotension **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Tachycardia and hypertension — Wrong. Opposite.

609. A man who ate honey from rhododendron nectar develops dizziness, a slow pulse and low blood pressure. Which toxin is responsible, and how does it act?

611. How does aconitine act, and which plants contain it?
   - **A.** It acts at nicotinic receptors; hemlock — Wrong. Coniine.

   - **C.** It blocks sodium channels; pufferfish — Wrong. Tetrodotoxin.
   - **D.** It blocks the sodium-potassium pump; foxglove — Wrong. Cardiac glycosides.

### Tumor lysis syndrome and heparin

681. A patient with Burkitt lymphoma starts chemotherapy and develops weakness, arrhythmia and acute kidney injury. Which electrolyte pattern is expected?
   - **A.** ↓ K+, ↓ phosphate, ↑ Ca2+ — Wrong. Opposite.
   - **B.** ↑ Ca2+ and ↓ uric acid — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** ↓ Na+ only — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Normal electrolytes with high LDH only — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** ↑ K+, ↑ phosphate, ↓ Ca2+, ↑ uric acid **(Correct)** — Correct. Tumor lysis syndrome ("BreaK the CUP").
   - Page: 440

682. Why does hypocalcemia occur in tumor lysis syndrome?
   - **A.** PTH is suppressed by tumor cells — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Calcium moves into tumor cells — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Calcium is lost in urine — Wrong. Not the mechanism.
   - **D.** Released phosphate binds and sequesters calcium **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Vitamin D is destroyed — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 440

683. Which drug converts uric acid to allantoin to prevent kidney injury in tumor lysis?
   - **A.** Probenecid — Wrong. Uricosuric; not used here.
   - **B.** Rasburicase **(Correct)** — Correct. Recombinant uricase.
   - **C.** Allopurinol — Wrong. Inhibits xanthine oxidase.
   - **D.** Colchicine — Wrong. Gout flares.
   - **E.** Febuxostat — Wrong. Inhibits xanthine oxidase.
   - Page: 440

   - **A.** Factors II, VII, IX, X synthesis — Wrong. Warfarin.

   - **C.** Factor XIII — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Platelet COX — Wrong. Aspirin.
   - **E.** Factors V and VIII — Wrong. Protein C.
   - Page: 440

   - **A.** Warfarin — Wrong. Teratogenic.
   - **B.** Dabigatran — Wrong. Not preferred.
   - **C.** Low-molecular-weight heparin **(Correct)** — Correct. Does not cross the placenta.
   - **D.** Apixaban — Wrong. Not preferred.
   - **E.** Rivaroxaban — Wrong. Not preferred.
   - Page: 440

686. Unfractionated heparin is monitored with which test?
   - **A.** PT/INR — Wrong. Warfarin.
   - **B.** Bleeding time — Wrong. Platelets.
   - **C.** Platelet count only — Wrong. Monitors HIT, not effect.
   - **D.** PTT **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Thrombin time only — Wrong. Not standard.

   - **B.** Tranexamic acid — Wrong. Antifibrinolytic.

   - **E.** Marrow suppression by heparin — Wrong. Not correct.

701. Warfarin inhibits which enzyme?

   - Page: 441

703. Which mnemonic links warfarin to its lab test?
   - **A.** "Don't Trast HER" — Wrong. Trastuzumab.
   - **B.** "Taxes stabilize society" — Wrong. Taxanes.
   - **C.** "When heparin is BAD" — Wrong. Direct thrombin inhibitors.
   - **D.** "Play Table Tennis inside" — Wrong. PTT/intrinsic.
   - **E.** "The ex-PresidenT went to war(farin)" **(Correct)** — Correct. Extrinsic pathway, PT.
   - Page: 441

704. Why is warfarin contraindicated in pregnancy?
   - **A.** It causes HIT — Wrong. Heparin.
   - **B.** It crosses the placenta and is teratogenic **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** It causes maternal hypertension — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** It is ineffective in pregnancy — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** It is cleared too quickly — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 441

705. Why is there an initial hypercoagulable state when warfarin is started?
   - **A.** Factor VIII rises — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Antithrombin falls — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Platelets are activated — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Protein C has a shorter half-life than factors II and X, so it falls first **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Fibrinogen increases — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 441

706. Why is heparin used as a "bridge" when starting warfarin?
   - **A.** It is required for warfarin metabolism — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** It prevents HIT — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** It reverses warfarin — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** It speeds warfarin absorption — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** It anticoagulates during the transient hypercoagulable state and lowers skin necrosis risk **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 441

707. Warfarin is metabolized mainly by which enzyme, making it prone to drug interactions?
   - **A.** Xanthine oxidase — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Alcohol dehydrogenase — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** CYP2C9 **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** CYP3A4 only — Wrong. Not the listed enzyme.
   - **E.** TPMT — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 441

708. Which is an indication for warfarin rather than a DOAC?
   - **A.** HIT — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** A mechanical heart valve **(Correct)** — Correct. DOACs are not used with mechanical valves.
   - **C.** Pregnancy — Wrong. Warfarin is contraindicated.
   - **D.** Atrial fibrillation without valve disease — Wrong. DOACs are suitable.
   - **E.** Uncomplicated DVT — Wrong. DOACs are suitable.
   - Page: 441

709. Compared with heparin, warfarin acts:
   - **A.** In the liver, with slow onset and a duration of days **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Monitored by PTT — Wrong. Heparin.
   - **C.** In the blood, with rapid onset — Wrong. Heparin.
   - **D.** Parenterally, for hours — Wrong. Heparin.
   - **E.** Without crossing the placenta — Wrong. Warfarin crosses.

710. Which direct thrombin inhibitor is used in patients with HIT?

   - **B.** Rivaroxaban — Wrong. Xa inhibitor.
   - **C.** Argatroban **(Correct)** — Correct. Also bivalirudin; "when heparin is BAD."
   - **D.** Warfarin — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Enoxaparin — Wrong. LMWH.
   - Page: 441

711. Apixaban, edoxaban and rivaroxaban act by:
   - **A.** Directly inhibiting factor Xa **(Correct)** — Correct. "Ban" factor Xa.
   - **B.** Blocking P2Y12 — Wrong. Clopidogrel.
   - **C.** Activating antithrombin — Wrong. Heparin.
   - **D.** Directly inhibiting thrombin — Wrong. Dabigatran.
   - **E.** Inhibiting vitamin K epoxide reductase — Wrong. Warfarin.

712. Which statement about direct oral anticoagulants is correct?
   - **A.** They are preferred in pregnancy — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** They should not be used in patients with mechanical heart valves **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** They are reversed by protamine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** They require INR monitoring — Wrong. Usually no monitoring.
   - **E.** They act through antithrombin — Wrong. Direct action.
   - Page: 441

713. A patient on dabigatran has a major bleed. Which reversal agent is used?

   - **B.** Protamine — Wrong. Heparin.
   - **C.** Desmopressin — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Andexanet alfa — Wrong. Xa inhibitors.
   - **E.** Vitamin K — Wrong. Warfarin.

714. Andexanet alfa reverses which drugs?
   - **A.** Warfarin — Wrong. Vitamin K, PCC.
   - **B.** Dabigatran — Wrong. Idarucizumab.
   - **C.** Heparin — Wrong. Protamine.
   - **D.** Apixaban and rivaroxaban **(Correct)** — Correct. Recombinant inactive factor Xa decoy.
   - **E.** Clopidogrel — Wrong. Not correct.

715. A patient on warfarin has an INR of 9 with intracranial hemorrhage. Which is the best immediate treatment?
   - **A.** Vitamin K alone — Wrong. Too slow.
   - **B.** Platelet transfusion — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Protamine — Wrong. Heparin.
   - **D.** Idarucizumab — Wrong. Dabigatran.
   - **E.** PCC (or FFP) plus vitamin K **(Correct)** — Correct.

716. Why does protamine bind heparin?
   - **A.** Protamine is a vitamin K analog — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Protamine is positively charged and heparin is negatively charged **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Protamine activates factor X — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Protamine degrades antithrombin — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Protamine is an antibody to heparin — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 442

717. Warfarin increases which coagulation test?
   - **A.** Platelet count — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Bleeding time — Wrong. Platelets.
   - **C.** D-dimer — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** PT/INR **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** PTT alone — Wrong. Heparin.
   - Page: 441

718. Warfarin skin necrosis is caused by:
   - **A.** Allergic vasculitis — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** HIT antibodies — Wrong. Heparin.
   - **C.** Small-vessel microthrombosis during early protein C depletion **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Infection — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Calcium deposition — Wrong. Calciphylaxis.

719. Which is a clinical use of direct Xa inhibitors?
   - **A.** Reversal of heparin — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Mechanical heart valves — Wrong. Not used.
   - **C.** DVT/PE treatment and stroke prophylaxis in atrial fibrillation **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Treatment of hemophilia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Treatment of TTP — Wrong. Not correct.

   - **C.** Bivalirudin — Wrong. IV.

   - **E.** Warfarin **(Correct)** — Correct.

### Antiplatelets, thrombolytics, and cancer drug targets

721. Aspirin irreversibly inhibits which enzyme?

   - **E.** Pulmonary fibrosis — Wrong. Bleomycin.

   - **E.** Aspirin — Wrong. COX.

   - **D.** Hepatotoxicity — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Bleeding and thrombocytopenia **(Correct)** — Correct.

727. Thrombolytics such as alteplase cause which laboratory changes?

   - **E.** ↑ PT and ↑ PTT with no change in platelet count **(Correct)** — Correct.

728. A patient with an acute ischemic stroke 2 hours ago is being considered for alteplase. Which history is a contraindication?

   - **B.** Hyperlipidemia — Wrong. Not a contraindication.
   - **C.** Previous intracranial hemorrhage **(Correct)** — Correct. Also active bleeding, recent surgery, bleeding diathesis and severe hypertension.

   - **E.** Idarucizumab — Wrong. Dabigatran.

   - **A.** They do not require antigen expression — Wrong. They do.

   - **E.** They avoid all toxicity — Wrong. Not correct.

   - **A.** Rb and p53 **(Correct)** — Correct.

   - **E.** HER2 and EGFR — Wrong. Not correct.

### Antitumor antibiotics and antimetabolites

741. A man treated for testicular cancer develops progressive dyspnea and dry cough. CT shows interstitial fibrosis. Which drug is responsible?

   - **C.** Bleomycin **(Correct)** — Correct. Also skin hyperpigmentation.
   - **D.** Etoposide — Wrong. Myelosuppression.
   - **E.** Doxorubicin — Wrong. Cardiomyopathy.

   - **B.** Rasburicase — Wrong. Tumor lysis.
   - **C.** Amifostine — Wrong. Nephrotoxicity.

   - **E.** Blocking BCR-ABL — Wrong. Imatinib.

   - **E.** Pericarditis only — Wrong. Not correct.

   - **E.** 5-FU — Wrong. S phase.

### Parkinson disease and neurodegenerative disease therapy

901. Why is carbidopa given with levodopa?
   - **A.** To block MAO-B — Wrong. Selegiline.
   - **B.** To inhibit central COMT — Wrong. Tolcapone.
   - **C.** To activate dopamine receptors directly — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** To increase dopamine release — Wrong. Amantadine.
   - **E.** To inhibit peripheral DOPA decarboxylase, increasing brain L-DOPA and reducing nausea **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 563

902. Unlike dopamine, L-DOPA:
   - **A.** Acts directly on D2 receptors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Is an MAO-B inhibitor — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Cannot cross the blood-brain barrier — Wrong. Reversed.
   - **D.** Crosses the blood-brain barrier and is converted to dopamine in the CNS **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Is not metabolized — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 564

903. A patient with long-standing Parkinson disease has periods of good mobility alternating with sudden immobility as each dose wears off. This is:
   - **A.** Serotonin syndrome — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Tardive dyskinesia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Akathisia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** The "on-off" phenomenon **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Neuroleptic malignant syndrome — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 564

904. Which drug blocks peripheral COMT to prevent L-DOPA conversion to 3-O-methyldopa but does not cross the BBB?
   - **A.** Tolcapone — Wrong. Crosses the BBB.
   - **B.** Selegiline — Wrong. MAO-B.
   - **C.** Entacapone **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Carbidopa — Wrong. DOPA decarboxylase.
   - **E.** Amantadine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 563

905. Which COMT inhibitor also acts centrally?
   - **A.** Rasagiline — Wrong. MAO-B.
   - **B.** Entacapone — Wrong. Peripheral only.
   - **C.** Tolcapone **(Correct)** — Correct. "Tolcapone pays the toll and can cross the BBB."
   - **D.** Pramipexole — Wrong. Agonist.
   - **E.** Benztropine — Wrong. Antimuscarinic.
   - Page: 563

906. Selegiline and rasagiline act by:
   - **A.** Selectively inhibiting MAO-B, preventing dopamine breakdown to DOPAC **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Agonizing dopamine receptors — Wrong. Pramipexole.
   - **C.** Inhibiting DOPA decarboxylase — Wrong. Carbidopa.
   - **D.** Blocking muscarinic receptors — Wrong. Benztropine.
   - **E.** Inhibiting COMT — Wrong. Entacapone, tolcapone.
   - Page: 563

907. A 55-year-old on pramipexole for Parkinson disease begins gambling compulsively. What drug class causes this?
   - **A.** NMDA antagonists — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Non-ergot dopamine agonists **(Correct)** — Correct. Impulse control disorder.
   - **C.** COMT inhibitors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** MAO-B inhibitors — Wrong. Not the listed effect.
   - **E.** Antimuscarinics — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 563

908. A patient on levodopa develops troublesome dyskinesias. Which drug reduces them and may cause livedo reticularis?
   - **A.** Selegiline — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Entacapone — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Benztropine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Amantadine **(Correct)** — Correct. Increases dopamine release and decreases reuptake.
   - **E.** Bromocriptine — Wrong. Ergot.
   - Page: 563

909. Benztropine is most effective for which Parkinson symptoms?
   - **A.** Tremor and rigidity **(Correct)** — Correct. Little effect on bradykinesia.
   - **B.** Postural instability — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Bradykinesia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Hallucinations — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Dementia — Wrong. May worsen.
   - Page: 563

910. Which dopamine agonist is an ergot derivative rarely used because of toxicity?
   - **A.** Pramipexole — Wrong. Non-ergot.
   - **B.** Amantadine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Bromocriptine **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Ropinirole — Wrong. Non-ergot.
   - **E.** Rasagiline — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 563

911. Which treatment helps advanced Parkinson disease?
   - **A.** Deep brain stimulation of the STN or GPi **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Thalamic ablation of the VPL — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Hippocampal resection — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Corpus callosotomy — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Cerebellar stimulation — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 563

912. Which first-line therapy is preferred in younger patients with Parkinson disease?
   - **A.** Levodopa/carbidopa — Wrong. Older patients.
   - **B.** Benztropine — Wrong. Not first.
   - **C.** Amantadine — Wrong. Not first.
   - **D.** Tolcapone — Wrong. Not first.
   - **E.** Non-ergot dopamine agonists **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 563

913. A woman with Alzheimer disease starts donepezil. Which patients should not receive it?
   - **A.** Those with glaucoma only — Wrong. Not listed.
   - **B.** Those with cardiac conduction abnormalities **(Correct)** — Correct. AChE inhibitors cause bradycardia.
   - **C.** Those with hypertension — Wrong. Not listed.
   - **D.** Those with diabetes — Wrong. Not listed.
   - **E.** Those with asthma only — Wrong. Not listed.
   - Page: 564

914. Which AChE inhibitors are first-line for Alzheimer disease?
   - **A.** Physostigmine only — Wrong. Not listed.
   - **B.** Donepezil, rivastigmine and galantamine **(Correct)** — Correct. "Dona Riva forgot the gala."
   - **C.** Neostigmine and pyridostigmine — Wrong. Peripheral.
   - **D.** Edrophonium — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Echothiophate — Wrong. Glaucoma.
   - Page: 564

915. Memantine helps moderate to advanced dementia by:
   - **A.** Blocking VMAT — Wrong. Tetrabenazine.
   - **B.** Inhibiting MAO-B — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Agonizing dopamine receptors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Inhibiting acetylcholinesterase — Wrong. Donepezil.
   - **E.** Antagonizing NMDA receptors to prevent Ca2+-mediated excitotoxicity **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 564

916. Riluzole increases survival in ALS by:
   - **A.** Removing antibodies — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Decreasing glutamate excitotoxicity **(Correct)** — Correct. "Treat Lou Gehrig disease with riLouzole."
   - **C.** Replacing SMN protein — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Blocking acetylcholinesterase — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Antagonizing dopamine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 564

917. A man with Huntington disease has disabling chorea. Which drug reduces it by inhibiting VMAT?
   - **A.** Donepezil — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Riluzole — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Memantine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Levodopa — Wrong. Worsens chorea.
   - **E.** Tetrabenazine or deutetrabenazine **(Correct)** — Correct. Also tardive dyskinesia.
   - Page: 564

918. Common adverse effects of levodopa include:
   - **A.** Hypertension and bradycardia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Weight loss and kidney stones — Wrong. Topiramate.
   - **C.** Nausea, hallucinations and postural hypotension **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Agranulocytosis — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Gingival hyperplasia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 564

919. Adverse effects of AChE inhibitors for dementia include:
   - **A.** Nausea, dizziness and insomnia **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Dry mouth and constipation — Wrong. Anticholinergic.
   - **C.** Hyperglycemia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Hair loss — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Tachycardia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 564

920. MAO-B inhibitors improve symptoms in patients with:
   - **A.** Chorea — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Dementia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Hallucinations — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Cyclic fluctuations in levodopa efficacy (on/off) **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Tremor only — Wrong. Not the listed indication.

### Local and general anesthetics

921. Which local anesthetic is an amide?
   - **A.** Chloroprocaine — Wrong. Ester.
   - **B.** Cocaine — Wrong. Ester.
   - **C.** Tetracaine — Wrong. Ester.
   - **D.** Lidocaine **(Correct)** — Correct. Amides have two i's in their names.
   - **E.** Benzocaine — Wrong. Ester.
   - Page: 565

922. A patient allergic to procaine-type esters needs a local anesthetic. Which should be chosen?
   - **A.** Bupivacaine **(Correct)** — Correct. If allergic to esters, give amides.
   - **B.** Tetracaine — Wrong. Ester.
   - **C.** Chloroprocaine — Wrong. Ester.
   - **D.** Cocaine — Wrong. Ester.
   - **E.** Benzocaine — Wrong. Ester.
   - Page: 565

923. Local anesthetics block conduction by binding:
   - **A.** K+ channels on the outer membrane — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Nicotinic receptors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Ligand-gated Cl− channels — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** The inner portion of voltage-gated Na+ channels **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Ca2+ channels at the NMJ — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 565

924. Why is more local anesthetic needed to drain an abscess?
   - **A.** Acidic tissue charges the anesthetic, so it cannot penetrate the membrane **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Bacteria degrade the drug — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Inflammation increases blood flow only — Wrong. Not the listed reason.
   - **D.** Neurons fire less — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** The drug is bound by pus — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 565

925. Epinephrine is added to lidocaine to:
   - **A.** Speed metabolism — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Prolong the block by reducing systemic absorption **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Prevent methemoglobinemia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Reduce pain of injection — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Increase systemic absorption — Wrong. Opposite.
   - Page: 565

926. Local anesthetics block sensation in which order?
   - **A.** Pressure, touch, temperature, pain — Wrong. Reversed.
   - **B.** Temperature, pressure, pain, touch — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Touch, pain, pressure, temperature — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** All at once — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Pain, temperature, touch, pressure **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 565

927. After a teething gel containing benzocaine, an infant becomes cyanotic with chocolate-colored blood and normal PaO2. Diagnosis?
   - **A.** Methemoglobinemia **(Correct)** — Correct. Benzocaine and prilocaine.
   - **B.** Cyanide poisoning — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Carbon monoxide poisoning — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Sulfhemoglobinemia — Wrong. Not listed.
   - **E.** Congenital heart disease — Wrong. Not correct.

928. Which local anesthetic causes severe cardiovascular toxicity?

   - **B.** Bupivacaine **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Lidocaine — Wrong. Less toxic.
   - **D.** Benzocaine — Wrong. Methemoglobinemia.
   - **E.** Tetracaine — Wrong. Not listed.

929. Nitrous oxide has rapid induction and recovery because it has:

   - **C.** High lipid solubility — Wrong. Potency.
   - **D.** Low solubility in blood **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Low MAC — Wrong. N2O has a very high MAC.

930. Isoflurane is more potent than nitrous oxide because it has:
   - **A.** Higher blood solubility only — Wrong. Not the listed reason.
   - **B.** Higher MAC — Wrong. Opposite.
   - **C.** Higher lipid solubility and lower MAC **(Correct)** — Correct. Potency = 1/MAC.
   - **D.** Faster elimination — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Lower lipid solubility — Wrong. Opposite.

931. MAC is defined as the alveolar concentration that:

   - **D.** Causes amnesia in 50% — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Prevents movement to a noxious stimulus in 50% of subjects **(Correct)** — Correct.

932. Inhaled anesthetics have which effect on the brain?

   - **B.** No effect — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** ↓ ICP — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** ↑ cerebral blood flow (↑ ICP) and ↓ metabolic rate **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** ↑ metabolic rate — Wrong. Opposite.

933. Why should nitrous oxide be avoided in a patient with a pneumothorax?
   - **A.** It is highly potent — Wrong. Very low potency.
   - **B.** It causes methemoglobinemia — Wrong. Not correct.

   - **D.** It increases ICP only — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** It causes malignant hyperthermia — Wrong. Not correct.

934. Which IV agent is most commonly used for induction of anesthesia?

   - **B.** Thiopental — Wrong. Not listed.
   - **C.** Propofol **(Correct)** — Correct. Potentiates GABA-A; causes hypotension and respiratory depression.
   - **D.** Midazolam — Wrong. Not most common.

   - **C.** Nitrous oxide — Wrong. Not IV.
   - **D.** Propofol — Wrong. Lowers BP.
   - **E.** Ketamine — Wrong. Raises BP.

936. A child given an IV anesthetic for fracture reduction has increased heart rate and BP and later reports vivid dreams. Which drug was used?
   - **A.** Ketamine **(Correct)** — Correct. NMDA antagonist; sympathomimetic and psychotomimetic.
   - **B.** Etomidate — Wrong. Neutral.
   - **C.** Fentanyl — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Propofol — Wrong. Lowers BP.
   - **E.** Midazolam — Wrong. Not correct.

937. Ketamine is useful in patients with bronchospasm because it:
   - **A.** Lowers cerebral blood flow — Wrong. Raises it.

   - **C.** Lowers heart rate — Wrong. Raises it.
   - **D.** Is a β-blocker — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Causes bronchodilation **(Correct)** — Correct.

938. Cocaine as a local anesthetic can cause:
   - **A.** Adrenal insufficiency — Wrong. Etomidate.
   - **B.** Hypertension and arrhythmias **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Methemoglobinemia — Wrong. Benzocaine.
   - **D.** Bradycardia only — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Malignant hyperthermia — Wrong. Not correct.

939. Local anesthetics work best in which neurons?
   - **A.** Resting neurons — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Upper motor neurons — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Only sympathetic neurons — Wrong. Not correct.

   - **E.** Rapidly firing neurons **(Correct)** — Correct.

940. A common adverse effect of inhaled anesthetics is:
   - **A.** Increased muscle tone — Wrong. Decreased tone.
   - **B.** Bradykinesia — Wrong. Not correct.

### Neuromuscular blockers, malignant hyperthermia, and muscle relaxants

941. Succinylcholine causes paralysis by:
   - **A.** Activating GABA-B — Wrong. Baclofen.
   - **B.** Inhibiting ACh release — Wrong. Botulinum.
   - **C.** Blocking ryanodine receptors — Wrong. Dantrolene.
   - **D.** Competitively blocking Nm receptors — Wrong. Nondepolarizing.
   - **E.** Strongly agonizing Nm receptors, causing sustained depolarization **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 566

942. A patient remains paralyzed for hours after a standard dose of succinylcholine. Which deficiency is likely?
   - **A.** Carnitine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** G6PD — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Ryanodine receptor — Wrong. Malignant hyperthermia.
   - **D.** Acetylcholinesterase — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Pseudocholinesterase **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 566

943. In phase I succinylcholine blockade, giving neostigmine will:
   - **A.** Reverse the block — Wrong. Phase II.
   - **B.** Cause hypokalemia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Have no effect — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Cause malignant hyperthermia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Potentiate the block **(Correct)** — Correct. No antidote for phase I.
   - Page: 566

944. A burn patient given succinylcholine develops peaked T waves and arrhythmia. Mechanism?
   - **A.** Hypermagnesemia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Hyperkalemia from sustained depolarization **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Hypocalcemia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Malignant hyperthermia only — Wrong. Not the immediate cause.
   - **E.** Hypokalemia — Wrong. Opposite.
   - Page: 566

   - **B.** Sugammadex **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Naloxone — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Dantrolene — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Atropine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 566

946. Why is glycopyrrolate given with neostigmine when reversing a nondepolarizing blocker?
   - **A.** To increase nicotinic effects — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** To provide sedation — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** To speed reversal — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** To prevent muscarinic effects such as bradycardia **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** To prevent malignant hyperthermia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 566

947. Neuromuscular blockers are selective for which receptor?
   - **A.** Nn receptors in autonomic ganglia — Wrong. Spared.
   - **B.** Nm nicotinic receptors at the NMJ **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** NMDA receptors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** GABA-A receptors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** M2 receptors — Wrong. Not correct.

948. Shortly after induction with sevoflurane and succinylcholine, a patient develops jaw rigidity, rising end-tidal CO2, tachycardia and fever. Which genetic change causes susceptibility?

   - **C.** RYR1 or dihydropyridine receptor mutations **(Correct)** — Correct. Malignant hyperthermia, autosomal dominant.
   - **D.** Dystrophin deletion — Wrong. Not listed.
   - **E.** Pseudocholinesterase deficiency — Wrong. Prolonged paralysis.

   - **D.** Dantrolene **(Correct)** — Correct. Ryanodine receptor antagonist.
   - **E.** Sugammadex — Wrong. Not correct.

   - **B.** Thyroid storm — Wrong. Not correct.

   - **D.** Hypothalamic set point elevation — Wrong. Fever.
   - **E.** Dopamine blockade — Wrong. NMS.

   - **D.** Mannitol — Wrong. Osmotic.

   - **A.** Loratadine — Wrong. Second generation.
   - **B.** Cetirizine — Wrong. Second generation.
   - **C.** Fexofenadine — Wrong. Second generation.
   - **D.** Desloratadine — Wrong. Second generation.
   - **E.** Diphenhydramine **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 704

1166. Which is a use of first-generation antihistamines?
   - **A.** Asthma attacks — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Motion sickness **(Correct)** — Correct. Also sleep aid and vomiting in pregnancy.
   - **C.** Peptic ulcer — Wrong. H2-blockers.
   - **D.** Hypertension — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Glaucoma — Wrong. Can worsen it.
   - Page: 704

1167. An elderly man takes diphenhydramine for sleep and develops urinary retention and confusion. Why?
   - **A.** Serotonergic effect — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** β-blockade — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Antimuscarinic effects **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** NMDA blockade — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** α-agonist effect — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 704

1168. Why do second-generation antihistamines cause less sedation?
   - **A.** They are given at lower doses — Wrong. Not the reason.
   - **B.** They block H2 receptors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** They act only on mast cells — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** They are agonists — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** They enter the CNS less **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 704

1169. How do H1-blockers work at the receptor?
   - **A.** As partial agonists — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** As reversible inhibitors (inverse agonists) **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** By degrading histamine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** By blocking histamine synthesis — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** As irreversible antagonists — Wrong. Reversible.
   - Page: 704

1170. At high doses, dextromethorphan causes dissociative, ketamine-like effects through which mechanism?
   - **A.** Serotonin receptor blockade — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Dopamine release — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** GABA-A agonism — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** NMDA glutamate receptor antagonism **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** μ-opioid agonism — Wrong. Mild opioid effect.
   - Page: 704

1171. A man taking an SSRI uses large amounts of cough syrup and develops agitation, hyperthermia and clonus. Which drug in the syrup is responsible?
   - **A.** Dextromethorphan **(Correct)** — Correct. Serotonin syndrome.
   - **B.** Loratadine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Diphenhydramine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Pseudoephedrine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Guaifenesin — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 704

1172. Which drug can be given for dextromethorphan overdose?
   - **A.** N-acetylcysteine — Wrong. Acetaminophen.
   - **B.** Flumazenil — Wrong. Benzodiazepines.
   - **C.** Naloxone **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Atropine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Physostigmine — Wrong. Anticholinergics.
   - Page: 704

1173. Bronchoconstriction in asthma is mediated by which two pathways?
   - **A.** Sympathetic tone and histamine only — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Dopamine and serotonin — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Thyroid hormone and cortisol — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Renin and angiotensin — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Inflammation and parasympathetic tone **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 704

1174. A child with asthma has an acute attack. Which drug gives rapid relief?
   - **A.** Montelukast — Wrong. Maintenance.
   - **B.** Salmeterol — Wrong. Long-acting.
   - **C.** Omalizumab — Wrong. Maintenance.
   - **D.** Fluticasone — Wrong. Maintenance.
   - **E.** Albuterol **(Correct)** — Correct. Short-acting β2-agonist.
   - Page: 704

1175. Which adverse effects are common with β2-agonists?
   - **A.** Seizures at normal doses — Wrong. Theophylline toxicity.
   - **B.** Dry mouth only — Wrong. Muscarinic antagonists.
   - **C.** Tremor and arrhythmia **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Oral thrush and hoarseness — Wrong. Inhaled steroids.
   - **E.** Hepatotoxicity — Wrong. Zileuton.
   - Page: 704

1176. What is first-line therapy for chronic persistent asthma?
   - **A.** Cromolyn — Wrong. Rarely used.
   - **B.** Theophylline — Wrong. Narrow index.
   - **C.** Inhaled glucocorticoids **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Zileuton — Wrong. Not first-line.
   - **E.** Oral albuterol — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 704

1177. How do inhaled glucocorticoids reduce airway inflammation?
   - **A.** They inhibit PDE-4 — Wrong. Roflumilast.
   - **B.** They stabilize mast cells only — Wrong. Cromolyn.
   - **C.** They block leukotriene receptors — Wrong. Montelukast.
   - **D.** They inactivate NF-κB and inhibit synthesis of most cytokines **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** They bind IgE — Wrong. Omalizumab.
   - Page: 704

1178. A patient using a fluticasone inhaler develops white plaques in the mouth. How can this be prevented?
   - **A.** Use a spacer or rinse the mouth after use **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Add a β-blocker — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Use the inhaler more often — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Take it with food — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Switch to oral albuterol — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 704

1179. How do ipratropium and tiotropium cause bronchodilation?
   - **A.** They competitively block muscarinic receptors **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** They block leukotrienes — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** They block histamine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** They activate β2 receptors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** They inhibit phosphodiesterase — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 704

1180. Which muscarinic antagonist is long acting?
   - **A.** Scopolamine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Tiotropium **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Glycopyrrolate only — Wrong. Not the textbook answer.
   - **D.** Atropine — Wrong. Not inhaled for this.
   - **E.** Ipratropium — Wrong. Short acting.

### Leukotriene modifiers, biologics, COPD and pulmonary hypertension drugs

1181. How does montelukast work?
   - **A.** Inhibits 5-lipoxygenase — Wrong. Zileuton.
   - **B.** Blocks IgE — Wrong. Omalizumab.
   - **C.** Stabilizes mast cells — Wrong. Cromolyn.
   - **D.** Blocks CysLT1 leukotriene receptors **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Inhibits phosphodiesterase — Wrong. Theophylline.
   - Page: 704

1182. Montelukast is especially useful for which type of asthma?
   - **A.** Status asthmaticus — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Cardiac asthma — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Occupational asthma only — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Aspirin-induced and exercise-induced asthma **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** Eosinophilic asthma requiring biologics — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 704

1183. A patient on an asthma drug that inhibits 5-lipoxygenase needs liver enzyme monitoring. Which drug?
   - **A.** Cromolyn — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Omalizumab — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Zileuton **(Correct)** — Correct. Hepatotoxic.
   - **D.** Montelukast — Wrong. Receptor blocker.
   - **E.** Zafirlukast — Wrong. Receptor blocker.
   - Page: 704

1184. How does omalizumab work?
   - **A.** It binds unbound serum IgE and blocks its binding to FcεRI **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** It targets IL-4 receptor α — Wrong. Dupilumab.
   - **C.** It targets IL-5 — Wrong. Mepolizumab.
   - **D.** It stabilizes mast cells directly — Wrong. Cromolyn.
   - **E.** It blocks leukotrienes — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 704

1185. Omalizumab is used in which patients?
   - **A.** COPD with emphysema — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Allergic asthma with high IgE resistant to inhaled steroids and LABAs **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Pulmonary hypertension — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** All patients with mild asthma — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Aspirin-induced asthma only — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 704

1186. How does theophylline cause bronchodilation?
   - **A.** Muscarinic blockade — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Leukotriene blockade — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** β2 receptor activation — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** IgE binding — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Inhibition of phosphodiesterase, raising cAMP **(Correct)** — Correct. Also blocks adenosine.
   - Page: 704

1187. A patient on theophylline starts ciprofloxacin and develops seizures and arrhythmias. Why?
   - **A.** Ciprofloxacin is a stimulant — Wrong. Not the main reason.
   - **B.** Theophylline has a wide therapeutic index — Wrong. Narrow.
   - **C.** Ciprofloxacin is a PDE inhibitor — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Both drugs block adenosine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Inhibition of cytochrome P-450 raises theophylline to toxic levels **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 704

1188. A COPD patient has frequent exacerbations despite inhaled therapy. Which oral drug that inhibits PDE-4 can reduce exacerbations?
   - **A.** Roflumilast **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** Sildenafil — Wrong. PDE-5.
   - **C.** Montelukast — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Zileuton — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Theophylline — Wrong. Nonselective PDE inhibitor.
   - Page: 705

1189. How does cromolyn work?
   - **A.** It blocks muscarinic receptors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** It binds IgE — Wrong. Omalizumab.
   - **C.** It inhibits PDE — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** It blocks H1 receptors — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** It prevents mast cell degranulation **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - Page: 705

1190. A patient with severe eosinophilic asthma starts benralizumab. What is its target?
   - **A.** IL-5 receptor α **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **B.** IL-5 itself — Wrong. Mepolizumab and reslizumab.
   - **C.** IL-4 receptor α — Wrong. Dupilumab.
   - **D.** TNF-α — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** IgE — Wrong. Omalizumab.
   - Page: 705

1191. Dupilumab inhibits signaling of which cytokines?
   - **A.** TNF-α and IL-17 — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** IL-5 only — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** IL-2 and IFN-γ — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** IL-4 and IL-13 **(Correct)** — Correct. Targets IL-4 receptor α.
   - **E.** IL-1 and IL-6 — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 705

1192. A COPD patient has persistent dyspnea and exacerbations. Triple inhaled therapy includes which combination?
   - **A.** Antihistamine, decongestant and antitussive — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Theophylline, roflumilast and cromolyn — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Inhaled corticosteroid, long-acting β2-agonist and long-acting muscarinic antagonist **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Montelukast, zileuton and omalizumab — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** SABA, oral steroid and antibiotic — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 706

1193. How does the PCV20 vaccine protect against pneumococcal pneumonia?
   - **A.** It produces only IgM — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** It is a toxoid vaccine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** It covers fewer serotypes than PCV13 — Wrong. More.
   - **D.** It induces a T-cell–dependent IgG response that reduces nasopharyngeal colonization **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **E.** It is a live attenuated vaccine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 706

1194. How does bosentan treat pulmonary arterial hypertension?
   - **A.** Traps TGF-β molecules — Wrong. Sotatercept.
   - **B.** Competitively antagonizes endothelin-1 receptors **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** Blocks calcium channels — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Acts as a prostacyclin analog — Wrong. Epoprostenol.
   - **E.** Inhibits PDE-5 — Wrong. Sildenafil.
   - Page: 706

1195. Which monitoring is needed for bosentan?
   - **A.** Thyroid function — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Liver function tests **(Correct)** — Correct. Hepatotoxic.
   - **C.** Serum calcium — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** Serum potassium — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Blood glucose — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 706

1196. A man with pulmonary hypertension on sildenafil develops chest pain. Which drug is contraindicated?
   - **A.** Nitroglycerin **(Correct)** — Correct. Risk of severe hypotension.
   - **B.** Heparin — Wrong. Not contraindicated.
   - **C.** Morphine — Wrong. Not contraindicated.
   - **D.** Metoprolol — Wrong. Not contraindicated.
   - **E.** Aspirin — Wrong. Not contraindicated.
   - Page: 706

1197. A patient receiving IV epoprostenol for PAH has flushing and jaw pain. What is the drug's mechanism?
   - **A.** Calcium channel blocker — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** PDE-5 inhibitor — Wrong. Sildenafil.
   - **C.** Prostacyclin analog causing direct vasodilation and inhibiting platelet aggregation **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Activin trap — Wrong. Sotatercept.
   - **E.** Endothelin receptor antagonist — Wrong. Bosentan.
   - Page: 706

1198. Sotatercept treats PAH through which mechanism?
   - **A.** Nitric oxide donation — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** PDE-5 inhibition — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Binding and trapping TGF-β family molecules to reduce arteriolar remodeling **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **D.** Prostacyclin receptor activation — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Endothelin receptor blockade — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 706

1199. Which adverse effects require monitoring with sotatercept?
   - **A.** Ototoxicity — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** Hypoglycemia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **C.** Hepatotoxicity only — Wrong. Bosentan.
   - **D.** Hyperkalemia and hyponatremia — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** Increased hemoglobin and thrombocytopenia with bleeding risk **(Correct)** — Correct. Also teratogenic.
   - Page: 706

1200. How do PDE-5 inhibitors help in pulmonary hypertension?
   - **A.** They block calcium entry directly — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **B.** They increase cGMP and prolong the vasodilatory effect of NO **(Correct)** — Correct.
   - **C.** They release histamine — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **D.** They block endothelin — Wrong. Not correct.
   - **E.** They lower cAMP — Wrong. Not correct.
   - Page: 706
